COUNTY PALATINH OF CHICSTHR, DISEASHS OF ANIMALS Aar, 1894. DOGS ORDER, 1906.

ItHOULATIONS A$ TO WICAHIN4 OF COLLARS BY DONS. NOTICE IS lIERES GIVEN that the Execu- tive Committee of the Local Authority of the County Palatine of Cheater, at a Meeting held on the Bth day of November, 1906 at Cheater Castle, made, in exercise of the powers conferred upon them by the Dogs Order. 19:16, the following reguiation as to the wearing of Collars by Dogs, such Regulation to come into operation on the let day of January. 1907 :

" No'boi;hall be allowed to be in or on any Highway or place of Public Resort unless such Dog is wearing, while in such Highway or place of Public Resort, a Collar with the name and aodress of the owner of such Dog Inscribed thereon or on a plate or badge attached thereto." HISGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the Chester County Cuuncil. Chester, 19th November, 1905. EXEMPTION FROM REGULATION. The foregoing Regulation does not apply to any pack of hounds or to any dog while being used for sporting purposes, or for the capture or destruction of vermin, or for the driving or tending of cattle or sheep. SBIZUHS OF DOGS WHERE REGULATIONS ARE CONTRAVBNED. . . . . _

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The King as Farmer.—The King is invariably very successful in taking prizes at the Fat Stock Shows, and his Majesty always makes a point of visiting the famous Christmas Show, held tinder the auspices of the Smith- field Club in London. His father, the late Prince Con- sort, joined the Club soon after his marriage,and he was 3 prize-winner in 1841, and in subsequent years. This set up a tradition which Queen Victoria and King Edward have since carried on. The Smithfield Club, which was started at the beginning of last century was a small concern uutil the patronage of Prince Concert greatly improved its fortunes, and now the prize-money offered at the Christmas Show, is usually over £l,OOO. A New Liability for Householders.—The inclusion of domestic servants within the Workmen's Compensation Act, will give Mary Jane numberless opportunities of threatening" to go to law with the master." Every householder who keeps servants, or a servant, or even employs a charwoman or temporary domestic will he liable to pay compensation for any personal injury arising" out of and in the course of the employment." If the maid runs a splinter into her hand when scrub- bing the floor, if she falls down the cellar stairs, or is knocked down in the street when posting a letter for her employers, or sustains a damaged eve when playing with the cnildren, then for such and all accidents which necessitate her "lying up for a longer than seven days" can Mary Jane demand dnmages. If she blows herself up with the gas cooker, or sets herself alight by pouring paraffin on the fire, or falls off the steps by her own carelessness, there would be a defence to an action for hamages, but such a defence is seldom successful, and the prudent housekeeper will do wisely to cover all risks by insurance which will probably not exceed 2s. od. or ss. a year, in small houses, where only one domestic is kept. The act will not in any case come into opera- tion until next July, so that householders will have plenty of time to decide whether ii is better to pay insurance, or try to do withobt dpmestics. Difficulties of Householders.—The extension of the Compensation Act to domestic service, is likely to prove injurious to the community of charwomen. All charwomen seem to have families dependent upon them and the compensation payable to employes with dependents is very much more than to those without. In the case of a fatal accident to a maidservant, the compensation might be no more than £lO or £2O, but with respect to a housekeeper, or charwoman, with a young family, it might be £l5O or more. Consequently there will be a disposition to avoid employing persons with such ties. In the case of incapacity arising from accident, the compensation is to be half the weekly "earnings "—that is for an indoor servant half the wages, end half the cost of her food, lodging, and laundry, etc. It is said that over 2,0000,000 persons, will be newly brought within scope of the Act, which, it is to be feared, will not only further estrange relations between mistress and servant, but also lend itself to the encouragement of malingering amongst a class in which such a disposition is already not altogether unknown. Two Crazes.—The well-to-do, and thoroughly up-to- date home, has been described as a sleeping place for the family, a warehouse for the furniture, and a prison for the servants. This is perhaps going a little too far, still it most be admitted that there is something in it all. The men folk of such an establishment are out at an early hour, travelling to town if they had business to attend to, or else golfing, shooting, motoring, or walk- ing. The children are out and about the greater part of the day, just coming in for meals, and a rest, whilst the mistress of the house is rarely to be found at home. Her mode of procedure is generally as follows— breakfast in bed, the late morning spent in shopping, i lunch at a club, the afternoon spent n paying calls, motoring, or skating. Then home to dress for dinner which is mostly taken at a restaurant, or in a friend's house, the evening being devoted to a theatre or dance. The middle and upper classes are possessed of two crazes at the moment, one is the mania for an out-of-door existence,and the other is a desire for perpetual motion. Science and Thought Reading.—Whether or not we believe in telepathy. or powers of second sight, it will be interesting to see what the Society for Physical Research thinks of the thought-transference perform- ances of Mr. and Mrs. Zancia, now taking place in London. The possible existence of "brain waves" was, like wireless telegraphy, suggested many years ago, and we have had various types of "thought-readers,' but in this case it is suggested that there is really a sixth sense, which enables Mr. Zancig to convey his thoughts to his wife, without resort to trickery, guesswork, or any known means of physical communication. If this can be proved to the satisfaction of the Physical Research Society, it will go far to removethe scepticism with which a great many people have hitherto regarded the °cult theories of hypnotists, spiritualists, and clair- vovants generally. The Scots Greys.—The removal from Edinburgh of the famous cavalry regiment of the Scots Greys will mean a great social loss to Scotland's capital, where the officers are most welcome at dances and other social gatherings. For over two hundred yeers, the regiment has been intimately associated with the Lothians, and it seems a pity that this should be severed, for apparently no other reason than the want of money to rebuild insantary barracks. When Norwich was threatened with a similar withdrawal, and for the same reason, the local authorities offered to rebuild the 1 barracks free of expense, to the government, and if such an offer were made, possibly it might keep the Scots Greys in Edinburgh. An influential movement is on foot to complete the monument on Calton Hill, which was to commerorate the slain at Waterloo, on the model of the Parthenon at Athens. That was begun in 1882, but never got further than the twelve of thirteen doric pillars, which has every visitor to Edin- bitrgh knows, stands in soli ory grandeur on the summit of Calton Hill. Perhaps as they have been left alone all these years, a few more or less would make little difference, and Edinburgh might consider whether it would not be better to ntilise the money for the rebuilding of Piershill barracks. Dress and the Seasons. —The time when summer and winter clothes were as far apart as the poles, is past and gone. For example, a smart afternoon toilette can be worn the whole year round, with the addition of a chiffon scarf, or feather boa in summnr, and furs in winter, while milliners appear to be disregarding the seasons more and more as time goes on. Fowers find a place in almost all the newest models, and taffetas brims are the order of the day. To the girl with only at small dress allowance at her disposal, this state of affairs is a fortunate one, for with a light weight tailor costume for the warmer season, and a heavy one for winter, she can muddle through" the seasons with the rest of her clothes very well. Padding and Bustles.—Lead in g dressmakers are slightly padding their dresses behind, and this no doubt accounts for the cry of alarmists that a revival of the bustle is at hand. As a matter of fact the padding is far removed from the old-fashioned bristle, which in all probability and taking well into account Dame Fashion's freaks, we shall never seen again. The clever dressmaker simply builds out bad figures by ran adriot arrangement of pleats and fullness and just a little wadding, and there is no possible doubt that even the average figure is all the b3tter for such consideration. A Peculiar Trimming.—Alany of the small fur boleros, which hail from Paris, are being trimmed in a curious fashion, viz., ornamented each aide in front with urge ribbon marguerites. On broad-tail, and Persian lamb, black daisies with dull gold centres find a place, while on chinchilla, silver ribbon shot with coral pink, is employed to build on the inevitable marguerite. On sable, white merging into pate orange yellow daisies are first favourities. In every instance shot ribbons are employed, the centre of the flowers being formed of knots of cord, whilst the wide spread out petals, which consist of loops of the ribbon, are applique to the fur. MAD GE.

FOR THAT WINTER COUGH.

Take Peps and Breath the Breath of the Rich Pine-Forest.

There are over forty millions of people in the British Isles, and it is declared that one in every ten is trouhled with a winter cough. It may be " only a simple cough," and people are too busy to trouble about bearable ailments now-a-days. But a winter cough is never so simple that you can afford to ignore it. It comes through neglecting a cold, and »La. 4 go away when spring comes round. But it has caused serious mischief to the delicate tissue of the lungs and bronchial tubes, and when the cough comes back next winter the mischief is aggravated. Then the cough becomes chronic,and lasts all the year round. It is just for this kind of trouble that Peps provide the ideal treatment. You dissolve a little Pen on your tongue and breathe into your weary lungs the healing breath of the rich pine woods. In your own home, or at yolr work, a handy box of peps endows you with the wonderful benefits of the pine-air treatment, which has made the pine-forest resorts of Switzerland and Nor- way so famous. Peps will cure the worst cough or cold. They are absolutely safe, being free from narcotics or dangerous drugs, and free from cubebs, stramonium, ammoniated compounds, and other irritating substances. Don't neglect that winter cough, and don't meddle with unsciantilic, useless, and often dangerous cough syrups and liquid mixtures, which do not and cannot reach your lungs when they are swallowed into your

2 McDOUGALL'S Patetit SELF-RAISING FLOUR iijEASIVaNy The Examiner, Saturday, December 15, 1906 LANCASHIRE COUNTY COUNCIL EXHIBITIONS AND SCHOLARSHIPS.

The amendment was defeated, only five voting for it, and the minutes were approved. DECREASE IN INMATES. . .

- Mr. FORSHAW said the rigorous policy pursued by the Board in discharging able-bodied people who were fit to maintain themselves had had its effect,the number of inmates having decreased by 50 as compared with the corresponding week last year.

The Lancashire County Council Education Committee has issued an attractive list of scholarships and exhibitions for competition during 1907. Exceptional opportunities are offered to scholars who are anxious to improve themselves educationally, the syllabus embracing elementary and secondary school subjects, special examinations for candidates intending to become pupil teachers, art, music, agriculture cotton technology. calico printing,and special scholarships for women. We would draw particular attention to the fact that the committee has decided to select a number (not exceed- ing 500) of boys and girls as candidates to be trained with a view of their being later apprenticed as pupil teachers in Public Elementary Schools iwithin the Administrative Coimty. The exhibitions, which are tenable for two year, provide free tuition, and (if necessary) travelling expenses. No payments will be made in respect of books or stationery. Exhibitioners must select for approval, Secondary Schools at which the fee charged does not exceed £6 per annum. Excep- tions may be made in the case of —(a) Pupils wko desire to remain at an approved Secondary Day School which they have attended for at least twelve months • or (b) Pupils resident in districts in which there is no school at which the fee charged is .C 6 or less than £0 per annum. No salary or other allowance for maintenance will be given. The school cen,tre, or classes must be approved' by the Lancashire Education Committee, and selected eandidates must be prepared to commence attendance in August or September, 1907. Candidates who are 14 years of age on the Ist day of August, 1907, will be required to attend the approved place of instruction for two years whilst those who are 15 years of age on the Ist day a August, 1907, will be required to attend the approved place of instruction for at least one year. No restrictions are laid down as to the residence of candidates, except that they must live within reason- able distance of the approved place of instruction, and of some of the County Elementary Schools at any one of which they would be willing later to be apprenticed. The examinations for these exhibitions will be held at various centres throughout the county on Saturday. April 20th, 1907, for compulsory subjects, and on Satur- day, April 27th 1907 for optional subjects. Conditions, regulations and particulars with reference to all the examinations may be obtained from Mr. H. Lloyd Snape, D., Sc., Ph. D., director of education, Preston.

COMMERCIAL FAlLURES.—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the total number of Commercial i failures recorded n England and Wales during the week ending Friday, December 7th, was 151, viz. New Bankruptcy Proceedings published in the London Gazette, 87 ; Deeds of Arrangement registered, 64. The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were : Bankruptcies, 91; Deeds of Arrangement, 96—total 187; being a decrease of 36. The total num- ber of Commereitd Failures recorded during the 49 weeks of the present year is 7,921 ; the total number recorded in the corresponding 49 weeks of last year was 8,518, showing a decrease of 697.

The magistrates, after a brief retirement, decided to dismiss the case.

Joseph Broady, landlord of the Cat and Lion, Stretton, was summoned for permitting drunkenness on his premises at 9.45 p.m. on November 27th. Mr. Churton said this was a very bad case. On November 27th, Acting-Sergeant Kennerley called at the Cat and Lion at 9.45. p.m., and saw fifteen persons standing round the bar. In te bar parlour he found a man named William Houghton sitting down in a perfectly helpless state of drunkenness. There was no drink in front of him, only a small jug of water on the table. The officer called the licensee's attention to Houghton, and Broady's answer was that Houghton had not been served for three-quarters of an hour, and that he had told Houghton to go. Broady tried to get Houghton out of the house, but could not do so as the man was unable to walk or do anything. Eventually Kennerley and a man named Brooks carried Houghton outside to his trap. They lifted him into the vehicle and the officer was about to take him to the Police Station at Stockton Heath wnen he was interfered with by two men. .

-Acting-Sergeant Kennerley gave evidence bearing ont Mr. Churton's statement. Dr. Parker who was called to see the man tleclared Houghton to be drunk and incapable. Mr. Browne said it would be idle on his part to say Houghton was not drunk. The milt question was as to the conduct of the landlord. It was quite true Houghton was sober at 4.M or 5 o'clock. He shot two trial birds, and also four out of the six in the match. He had some tea, and then unfortunately,some whisky and beer, with the result that he did fall sud- denly in a drunken condition. A well-known farmer borrowed Houghton's pony and trap the arrangement being that it was to be sent straight back. Unfortun- ately, Brooks, the man in charge, did not 4to straight back and the result was that 'Houghton had some drink whilst he was waiting for the vehicle, which did not arrive until shortly before Acting Sergeant Kennerley called at the Cat and Lion. Mr. Broady did not serve Houghton when he found what his condition was, but what was he to do ? If Mr. Broady had put him in the roadway he would have been equally liable, while if a policeman had been sent for, defendant would then have been summoned for permitting drunkenness. He (Hr. Browne) submitted that taking all the circumstances of the case into consideration, the justice of the case would be met by payment of the costs. The Bench retired to consider their verdict, and on their rettrrn said the defendant would be fined 411 s. and costs.

On the application of Mr. Churton, the advocate's fee was allowed.

William Houghton was then summoned for being found drunk on the liceiried premises of Mr.Broady. He pleaded not guilty. Mr. Browne, however, advised defendant to plead guilty, and he did so. He was tined ss. and costs.

Robert Brooks and John Russell were then summoned for having obstructed Kennerley while in the execution of his duty and were fined 2s. fid. and costs each.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Application for Infirmary Donation. A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday under the presi- dency of Mr. J. Billings. There were also present : Mrs. Clay. Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, Dr. Murray Messrs. A. J. Jolley, R. Forshaw, S. Davies, W. C. Brooks, 0. Mrither, 111. W. Seton, W. Flannagan, R. Bolton, A. Brooks, J. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (master) and A. Wright (superintendent of Pulgate schools.) DONATION TO THE N.S.P.C.C.

The Local Government Board wrote sanctioning the granting by the Guardians of a donation of five guineas per annum to the N.S.P.C. THE INFIRMARY EXTENSION.

A letter was read from Mr. W. Peter Rylands, chair- man of the Infirmary Extension Committee, appealing for a subscription from the Board in aid of that object. lie was sure the Board fully realized the value of the Infirmary to the town and district generally and the assistance it rendered to the Board itself in the treat- ment of many special cases. Mr. JOLLEY asked if the Guardians had power to sub- scribe to the fund.

The CLERK said he was not quite clear on the point. The Local Government Board might possibly look upon such an object as special because the Board undoubtedly benefited by the Infirmary which treated many cases that would otherwise Come to the Workhouse. The extension would have the effect of adding to the benefits they derived. It was decided to place the matter on the agenda for consideration at the next meeting.

CHRISTMAS BEER. IMPROVEMENTS IN THE LAUNDRY. At a meeting of the House Committee it was resolved that the usual Christmas extras and beer be given to the officers and inmates, and that the Master defray from petty cash the sum of 50s. to purchase toys for the children. At a subsequent meeting the question of providing additional laundry machinery WRS the subject of considerable discussion. The report of the first sub- committee was again submitted, as well as the individual views of the three Guardians appointed to visit Winwick Asylum in connection with the matter. Ultimately it was decided to first test the feeling of the committee as to the advisability of putting down additional machinery as the best solution of the difficulty, and this was carried by the casting vote of the Chairman. THE LIGHTING OF STREETS.

Warrington Tradesmen's Complaint.

Warrington tradesmen are up in arms against the lighting of the main streets of the town and vigorous complaints were made at the quarterly meeting of their Association on Tuesday. Mr. F. Pendlebury presided over a representative attendance. The CHAIRmaN said that in the matter of lighting the streetd the question was as to whether they could do anything to prevail upon the Corporation to light Sankey-street and other streets more in keeping with their importance as the principal streets of the town. 1 Mr. J. E. RICHARDSON voiced a grievance of the tradesmen in regard to the lighting of shops. He thought that in Sankey-street the trouble arose through the mains being old ones. Mr. IsturatWOOD said that on his premises he adopted the electric light but had reverted to gas as he could not get sufficient light from the newer system. His premises had to be lit all day and during the day time there was not sufficient pressure through the meter. With regard to the street at night time from Trinity Church to Cairo-street, Sankey-street -was as dark as pitch and manystimes he had discovered boys and girls lading in the doorways of the shops. Mr. COOPER thought the diffic'ulty arose in the day- time through the pressure of gas in the mains being reduced. He thought that the only way to light the streets properly would be to place gas-lamps between the electric light standards as the electric light was not reliable at all and frequently went out. Mr. RICHARDSON, in moving that letters of complaint be sent to the Electric Lighting Committee and Gas Committee with regard to the lighting of shops, said it seemed as though there was considerable rivalry between the two Committces, and that they as rate- payers wers suffering from the personal pique of mem- bers of the Council.

Mr. Richardson's motion was approved, and it was also decided to ask that the main streets of the town should be better lighted.

CHEAP TRANI FARES. SHOP ASSISTANTS' GRIEVANCES.

The meeting then proceeded to consider the question of the provision of facilities to enable shop assistants and juniorclerks to travel on the tram cars at a rectuced rate. _

The CHAIRMAN said it had occurred to the Executive that the present regulations with regard to cheap tram fares were not quite fair to girls,young clerks and boys yaw lived on the outskirts of the town. It was hardly right that they should be compelled to paithe full fare when working men who might earn £2 per week should be able to travel at a special rate. They thought some consideration ought to be shown to the various assistants.

Mr. CRITCHLICY thought tokens might be issued to juniors under 21 if their employers gave a written

Accidents. A serious accident ockurred at Latchford Locks on Monday morning. Williatn Thomas, a seaman on the s.s. Littlebourne, was in the hold of the boat storing. steam pipes, which were being lowered from the locks into the vessel, when one of them slipped out of the. slings and fell on his face, causing a large gash and a fracture of the jaw-bone. He was attended by Dr. Leadbetter and afterwards conveyed in the horse ambulance to the Infirmary, where ho was detained.— At about 1.45 on Monday afternoon, Mr. Richard Billlngton, a farmer, Stretton, was driving his pony and trap along Wilderspool-causeway when the pony stumbled, and he was thrown violently out of the trap. He was carried intci an adjoining house where he was attended by Dr. Langdale. He was afterwards conveyed to the Infirmary, in the police horse ambul- ance, where he was detained, suffering from a seve cut over the eye, and several other injuries.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

[By " Ovalist."]

The hope will be general in Warrington that the Northern Union Cup Competition when it arrives, will not take Warrington to Wigan this season. They have bee* well-beaten there twice already, and ou successive Saturdays too. Last week they met the Wiganers themselves and their fat 3 was even worse than the week before in the Lancashire Cup final. Twenty points to two points is not explained merely by the absence of Fish. In other respects the team was at full strength but the heart seemed to be cut of them. They never gave the impression that they were going to epeat their success over Wigan at Warrington on November 3rd. Wigan had something to wipe off the slate and they did it very thoronahly.

Warrington were let down forward pretty badly and the men gave nothing like the display they did against the Radgers. Their tacking was most feeble and at times the Wigan men ran through them in a fashion which showed there was a screw loose some- where. Jordan was not a great success at half and the same can be said of Hockenhall on the wing. At half-time they changed places, but the same remarks might be repeated. Taylor, too, was below form and the brightest work was done by Berry, Dickenson, aid Brooks. The Widnes man has been very heavily worked this season and appears to want a rest.

On the Wigan side, Anderson and Jenkins were very clever anti occasionally made the Warrington backs look rather small. Both frequently ran clean through the opposition and most of the scoring came from their efforts, backed up by some good goal-kicking by Leytham, who seemed out of place in the centre. The Wigan forwards again had the better of the Warrington men; and why the Club should keep on purchasing men like Cheetham, of Swinton, is a bit of a mystery. They must have too much capital, and believe footballers a sound investment. I ought to add that Sharrock, the one-time three-quarter, gave a dashing full back display, and frequently changed the aspect of the game by smart tactics.

On Monday, a somewhat re-arranged Warrington team went to Liverpool anti smote the poor City men in very severe fashion. They were expected to put about 50 points up, and in getting 47 they came very near, the mark. But for some careless goal-kicking the score would have taken Warrington's total past the 300 mark in the League. As it is they are six points short, with Runcorn next a point behind. At Liverpool, the Warrington men scored 13 tries. Brooks and Thomas getting three tries each. Only four goals were kicked, however, and it was a case, rather, of "too many cooks," &c. Thomas was about the best men on the field and had hard luck in not getting two more tries at least. The new half-ba3k, Jenkins, also scored a couple of tries, but spoiled a fair display by too much kicking. Generally, the Warrington men went in for tossing the ball about, so the game was always bright and open. The ground was too hard for severe tackling and no risks were A taken.

Liverpool got seven points, but made an awfully poor show. The team is very scraggy, and the men lacking in physique to a hopeless extent. The War- rington " A "team would make mincemeat of them,and what their prospects can be is a mystery. Already they have nearly 600 points against them,and ne'er a victory to cheer them on their troublous way. The City management made a mistake in tackling senior foot- ball. A season in the Lancashire Canibination would have brought valuable experience, and perhaps some encouraging results.

The " gate " at Liverpool was 255. ! If the Club holds on to the end of the season it will surprise most people. I hope, however, such will be possible to the plucky band of enthusiasts who are keen on establishing the Rugby game in Liverpool.

There was a fine crowd at Wilderspool notwith- standing the dismal day to witness the duel between the Combination Champions and the runners-up. Wigan had six men on who appeared in the first team's ranks on November 3rd, but victory rested with Warrington by 2 tries to nil. In the first half Wigan had the wind and attacked persistently, but met a fine defence in which Jenkins shone with smart clearances. On turning round the locals put the issue beyond doubt by rushing the Wigan defence in the first minute. Landor got both tries and shaped in nod style, while the young forwards were full of gnergy.

"I The return match with Hunslet will, take place to-day at Wilderspool and ought to result in a victory for the locals. Changes have been made in the home team and men like Fish, Isherwood, Boardman and Shugars will be missed.

AMATEUR COMEDIAN'S STORY

Face Became Blotchy from Use of Grease Paints—Lower Part was Covered with Red Pimples Doctor's Mixture had No Effect.

CURED BY THE CUTICURA REMEDIES

_ _ "I am an amateur comedian. The troupe, during last summer, gave some- thing like three ishows a week, and it was necessary for each one to make up his face with grease paints. About the beginning of July my face began to get blotchy and became worse and worse, till at the end of a week the lower part was covered with red pimples. I went to my doctor, who gave me some blood mixture, which I took for about a fort- night without effect. I then purchased some Cuticura Soap and Cuticura Oint- ment, and used them as directed. After a fortnight's usage the pimples began to dry up; after a month's treatment I was able to brush them off with a soft towel, they having become bits of dried-up white skin. I wish you to understand that I put this eruption down to the continual use of theatrical makeup and the hot weather. A. F. Trotman, 126 Barrows Road, Sparkhill, Birmingham, Feb. 12, 1906."

===== 0002601_19061215_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
[ALL RIGHTS RRIARVISD.

WRITTEN IN SCARLET.

By ALICE M A LTD MEADOWS, Author of "One Life Between," "A Million of Money," "The El:trerte Penalty," "For Love Of a Lady," "I Charge You Both," &a

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING 'CHAPTERS: It is the wedding eve of John Brandon, a .well-to-do country gentleman, and Celia Verde, daughter of Lady Caroline Verde. Celia had been engaged to John's brother Dick, a wild and reckless young man, supposed ,to have died abroad.' Celia admits that she loved her old sweetheart better than the new—in fact, that she does not love John at all. He, however, adoring her, promises .himself that he will win her love after marriage: , Then all is changed by the sudden reappearance of Dick, who meets his brother as he is going homewards. No other person has seen the wanderer .except Lady Caroline's maid, who takes him for a ghost. John Brandon lets his brother in by the library window, unseen by his servants. Dick laughs at the idea of John marrying Celia, who, he is certain, still loves himself. His careless taunts ,excite Joint to fury. and he strikes his brother violently on the head with a ruler, intending only tO disable, him until after the wedding takes place. To his horror, however, the signs of death are manifest. With quiet resolution he places the body in a large safe. At that moment the police arrive at the house in search of a convict who has just escaped from a neighbouring prison. They insist on examining the house, as the fugitive has been traced in that direction. Finally they open the safe. John Brandon is in an agony of fear. The safe is empty! Later on the convict knocks at the study window, and Brandon admits him. He explains that In Brandon's absence he entered, opened the safe, and discovered the body, .which he abstracted with the intention of changing clothes. He Was disturbed by the police, and hid the body hurriedly, and is now in the darkness unable to find the place again. A better plan of safety, *however, has now sug- gested itself. He is a gentleman born and bred, and he compels Brandon to introduce him to the wedding guests as Vernon Salisbury, an old friend, who had unexpectedly .turned up.

===== 0002601_19061215_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER X.

DIGNAL.

The world abounds in pretty girls and pretty women, but to every mah there is a prettiest. Olive, John Brandon's sister, was the prettiest girl in the whole wide world to Paul Clifford; the prettiest, the daintest, the dearest. He loved he/ very truly, very devotedly, very passionately. He had not yet asked her to be his wife, because, like most young lovers, he was timid. It seemed to him he was not half good enough for her a king, in his estimation, would scarcely have been her equal. But deep down in his heart he cherished the hope that some day he would be audacious enough to ask her to be his wife; and since there was no one to whom she was kinder than she was to him, he secretly entertained the hope that she might say yes.

"No man in the whole wide world is worthy of her," he said to himself. "That being the case, since if she weds it must be an inferior being, 'Why should it not be I?"

He was almost as excited about John Brandon's marriage as though he himself had been the prospective bridegroom. It seemed to him that the affair must help towards the -attainment of his heart's desire. John and Olive had been very, very much to one another, absolutely devoted, as folk had been fond of saying, but the advent a a young wife must put her nose out of joint; she would find herself de trop—out in the cold.

"And then, little darling sweetheart, I'll ask her to (bale to me," Paul said to himself.

All night ,long he had not slept. Olive had promised he should be her escort after the marriage ceremony wa. over; that he should lead her from the altar. It seemed to him that must be prophetic. • One marriage makes many. He rose early because he could not sleep. John had, of course, ordered the bouquets for the bridesmaids, but P-. had bad his way in that matter also, for he had interviewed the florist. Olive's posy, nice those of the rest of the bridesmaids, was to be of pink roses, but a sovereign had changed places from Paul's hand to his, and hers was to be more beautiful—each blossom more perfect than that of anyone else. "She won't guess, of course," he said to him- self ; "but I shall know, and that's enough for me." He dressed in a walking suit, and at five o'clock left his room for a tramp through the dew-laden lanes. There are times in most of our lives when a great restlessneas falls upon us. Paul Clifford was restless now. "She won't be up for hours," he said to him- self, "but I'll go and have a look at her windows. Some day, please Heaven, it will be our wedding morning. I shall be up and cut and prowling round her home then, and perhaps she'll be looking out too—just to see what sort of a day kind chance had given us." He tramped on merrily, his spirits on top notes, through the quiet, deserted-looking village. It quite surprised him, gave him a shock, when on passing the chemist's shop someone Caine out, almost ran into him. The man had nothing in his hands; for all that, Paul Clifford thought he had been getting medicine lie looked pale and nervous. "What's the meaning of this, Dignal ?" Paul asked. "You're not generally such an early bird, are you ?" The man, for some reason or other, looked vexed. The chemist, arrayed in a dressing-gown and list slippers, shut the door. "I don't generally feel as ill as I have all through the night, Mr. Clifford," the man returned, "or I'd not be out now. Some folk like the mirly morning. Give me the atmosphere about nine or ten o'clock, when the sun's had time to temper it. You're up betimes." • Paul Clifford laughed. "Couldn't sleep," he answered. "This wedding seems to have got on my nerves, though why it should I don't know, Pm sure. You're going, of course? Mr. Brara:on is such a friend of yours." A strange look crossed the man's face. "He's been the best friend I ever had, or am likely to nave," he answered. "I've, as you know—as everyone knows—had a rare lot of trouble; never able to do much work, and with a sick wife and sick children, all but one removed to a better land now—thank Heaven! for their sakes, Mr. Paul. Yes, Mr. John has been s. good friend to me. Heaven only knows what I should have done often if it hadn't been for iim. Well, the mouse helped the lion once, so it is said. Don't you trouble to walk with me, sir, I'm just going home, that's all." Paul paced on by his side. "Oh .I.l'll come with you," he answered. "I've nothing to do, and shall be glad of a chat. If you're making yourself some tea, I don't mind taking a cup, if you press me. What were you getting at the chemist's ? "

The man's face had fallen. He looked round, with something of helplessness in his glance, at the dew-laden fields and hedges. The, sun was rising, lending a rosy glow to nature; the birds were twittering in their nests. "I'm not feeling any too well, Mr. Clifford," the man returned. "Fits of twinges all over me ; rheumatism it may be, or neuralgia. I—l wasn't thinking of making tea; to tell you the truth, the doctor warned me against it. On second thoughts I won't interfere.

He heard the clocks strike six. It seemed to him that they struck under protest, as though they hardly thought it worth while to tell the time when no one was about to hear. It was a foolish fancy, of course, engendered by the almost absolute silence that reigned around. He was drawing nearer and nearer •to the home of his idol. He could see the verdant green of the parklands, the great trees which thrived so luxuriously in the more cultivated soil, the harsh note of a peacock screaming to his mate fell upon his ear. "What's the matter with Dignal ?" he said tc himself. "Why wouldn't he let me go home with him? 'He wasn't, of course, afraid in the way he said; but he was afraid, for all that." He whilked in the direction of John Brandon's house. He knew Olive sometimes rose very early, and strolled about the gardens. To-day, it was, of course, more than probable she might stop late in bed, resting herself. Still, there was the off-chance she might he'up. The off-chance sometimes comes off.

Be vaulted a gate and was in John Brandon's land. In a minute a couple of dogs came racing towards him, barking angrily at first, then jump- ing round him with joy, almost knocking him off his feet in the ardour of their welcome. "Down, Brutus! Down, Samba!" Paul said. "You're up early, old chaps. What's the meaning of it? Who is abroad—your master? If so, why are you not with him ? " The dogs stood back a little and barked loudly; then, seemingly without rhyme or reason, they raced off, deserting Paul Clifford altogether.. Paul went on. He had reached the lawn new. He could see Olive's window, and as he drew nearer and nearer his eyes fixed upon it. Oh. joy most wonderful, it was opened, and Oily. • looked out upon the Morning, in some sort of a white wrapper,. with her beautiful bright brown hr untmund.

The birds were still twittering in their nests. or hopping about near them. The rabbits, live ever' were very wideawake, scampering here and there, pausing suddenly .when they came across something that would make a succulent breakfast, sitting up in the prettiest, quaintest way to take it. No one else seemed about. Paul Clifford had the world, as it were, or so he thought, to him- self. ,And then suddenly, almost with a rush, as though he had intended to catch hold of him, a man came out from behind a hedge. . Paul Clifford recognised him at once. It was Chief Warder Clark from Dartmoor prison.

For a minute she did not see him, then aghi quickened his lace her eves met his. She coloured slightly, and put out her hand to close the windew. •

"Oh, please—please don't!" he said, his voice ringing very clearly through the crisp morning air. • "I want to talk to you. I have been lying awake all night thinking of you. I couldn't sleep. Couldn't you come Gown, just— just because it's Jack's wedding-day, td mark the occasion?"

He drew back evidently embarrassed, and touched his hat. "Beg pardon, Mr. Clifford," he said, "I thought—well, I was a fool, of course, to dream for a minute he'd come along stepping free and careless like that, but I thought it might be our man coming. You know one escaped last evening? You heard the guns of course? We've been up all night watching; getting, as we thought, on a scent, and drawing blank. I suppose he's got away. It was only to be imagined, him being so clever, that he would; or perhaps someone is habouring him. Gentle- man Jim, as he was called in prison, wouldn't be likely to lack friends, and so many are open to a bribe."

She pushed the French window open again. The sleeve of her white AN r.tpper fell back front her plump, round arm, faintly pink like a. baby's. He looked at it, and told himself he would have given ten years of his life to have kissed it. He was not the first man by a Ycry, very long way who had been moved to madness by the beauty of a woman's arm. ought to be asleep," she said, nhispering tha words down to him. "I shall look a wreck at the wedding, but I also have not been able to rest." _

Paul Clifford started slightly. Was that the explanation of Dignal's strange conduct?. Had the convict come to him, bribed him? Had he hidden him ? It really semed more than possible. The man also was very probably ill ; had, perhaps, injured himself in his escape. Paul Cliffori knew it was his duty to impart his suspicions to Chief Warder Clark. For all that, he did not do so.

"You will look a queen," he answered, "a fairy! Oh, Olive, come down, come down ! want "—he felt the blood surge to his brain—"l want to •ty good-morning." She laughed softly, the little teeth gleamim, between the red lips, her beautiful eyes dark and wide with some pleasurable emotion. "Say it, then," she answered, "I can hear. We are not so far apart as all that." He stretched up hi s arms, but, alas! 817le WtW far above him. "I want you here," he said, "down here by my side. Olive, I've so—l've something more to say to you than good-morning. I've wanted to say it for months and months, hut have not dared. I've tile courage now, perhaps, because the music of wedding bells. though they have not rung yet, is in the air. On, come down, dearest, come down!" She would have been less than a woman had she not understood. She looked at him and blushed. She looked still, and paled. Like most creatures feminine, she was a little afraid , of love, and surely that was what was to be offered to her—love and the prayer that she would pledge her troth to this young lover, that not so very long ahead she would become his wife. She planted her rounded elbows on the window- sill, with her pretty hands she made a resting- place for the dainty chin. If there had been a creeper up that part of the house, anything that would have given Paul --Clifford even a perilous foothold, he would have been at her side in a minute. Unfortunately for the modern Romeo, the wall was a blank cue.

It is a curious but undeniable fact that nearly everyone is disinclined to give a convict away. Whatever crime he may have committed. seems not to matter at all, so far as the aider and abettor is concerned. He has made a bold stroke for freedom. If the minions of the law can re-take him, well and good. Not one private individual out of fifty will help in the recapture. Paul Clifford glanced round carelessly, almost as though he expected to see the runaway. "Bribery or no bribery," he said, "you're sure to catch him sooner or later. What with your photographs of convicts, the impressions you take of their finger-tips, height, and so on, it seems to me they've very little chance of escape. So you've been up all night. Well, you look tired.'

Warder Clark sighed dolefully. "And am half dead, sir," he answered. "That's not to be wondered at since it's been a night full of dis- appointment, and the weather not too clement. Besides, I've a young wife at home that don't like being left, and whom, naturally enough, Mr. Clifford, I don't like leaving. Two or three times, too, we've thought we had our hands on the fellow. Ones it seemed certain he war hidden in a safe at Mr. John Brandon's, but It wasn't. Still, cold and hungry and baulked, I feel I can't help laughing when I think of the ten minutes we spent in Mr. Brandon's study. He won't tell you about it, take my word." Warder Clark laughed as he spoke; evidently something amused him very much. Paul Clifford looked mystified. "What's the joke?" he asked. "What do you mean by Mr. Brandon won't tell me of your visit to his house? I should think it very probable he won't. He won't think of much but his bride to-day; he'll hardly want to." Chief Warder Clark winked. "So I should have said twenty-four hours ago," he answered. "If anyone had made the observation to me, Does Mr. John Brandon truly and faithfully love Miss Verde, with a single heart and a sincere passion ? ' I should have said yes. But last night--It's too good a joke to keep wrapped up in one's own memory, but you won't let it go any further, Mr. Clifford ? " "Why, of course not," Paul Clifford returned. "Then it's like this, sir, Mr. John Brandon has two strings to his bow; one he's going ,to marry to-day, and one he said goodbye to last night. We nearly caught her. Mr. Brandon thought we had caught her, and a nice blue funk he was in too!" _ _

She smiled, half-nervously, half-teasingly. "I wonder what you Would hare to say to me ? " she asked. "Any way, surely it will keep until, after the wedding ? " He shook his head. "I want to tell you now," he answered, "and I think—oh, I hope you can guess what it is. Olive, won't you collie down ? I would call it out for all the world to hear only I'd rather whisper it. May I? Won't you come ?"

For a moment she looked irresolute, then once more she coloured. "Perhaps I will," she answered. "I won't promise, but you may -.iait if you like on the seat in the rosery. if I come at all, I will come there. I "—she laughed nervously—"hope it is sotnethiug very interesting you have to tell Inc. I'm afraid I shall be angry if it is not." She closed the window before he could answer, but he felt anything but downhearted for all that. He was to go to the seat in the rose garden and wait there for her. He was perfectly certain she would come, and then he would tell her the secret he could not any longer keep to himself—that he loved her—loved her, heart, body and soul; that he worshipped her and her only, and always would while life lasted, and after.

"Surely she knows," he said to himself, "and knowing, she would, not be so unkind as to make me hope only to send me away in despair. Oh, it is certain, quite certain, that soon I shall be the happiest, luckiest fellow in the whole wide world! "

An expression of anger crossed Paul Clifford's face. He could have knocked the man down for the lie.

"What an unwarrantable creation of your own brain!" he said, angrily. "There never has been never will be, but one woman in Mr. John Brandon's life. If I didn't know better, I should any you'd been drinking to make such a state- ment.

He walked on, and as he made his way to the rose garden his heart sang an anthem of joy, leaped and bounded in his bosom, his whole frame tingled with gladness. She was coining to him— his queen, his lady,, his sweetheart! Coining shyly, perhaps—that made her coming all the sweeter, maidenly modesty is always dear to the clean- souled man—but coming joyously for all that; a little afraid, but very, very happy.

The man looked sullen. "It's the truth," he said, "the absolute truth. Someone when we knocked at the door, had been hurriedly locked in the safe. Of course, we thought it was our man, but it wasn't; it was a lady. Mr. Brandon thought she was still there when we demanded that the safe should be opened." "And she wasn't, of course ? " "She wasn't," Warder Clark returned, "though where she had got to neither we nor Mr. Brandon knew. Take my word for it, he was more surprised /than we were to find the safe empty, aria he ,vasn't clever enough to hide it. If ever a man looked as though he was going to die of fright, he did. I've heard—of course its common gossip—that he was just mad for Miss Verde. Perhaps a man can be mad in two quarters." Paul Clifford frowned. "A man can only love one woman," he said, ,coldly, "and Mr. Brandon loves the woman who to-day will be his wife. Well, I suppose you've got your business to look after. Good-morning." "Good-morning, sir," Warder Clark answered, and returned to his hiding-place, laughing as went.

CHAPTER XII. THE GREEN-EYED MONSTER,

"What shall I say to her ? " he said to himself.• "My heart is so full. How can I put all my love into words ? It is impossible!" And then he smiled. Need he put it all into words ? Might not some of it—if she seemed kind, and surely she -,aard'be—he expressed in kisses, in the clasp of his arms around her ? He had always read that words were inadequate in the expression of love. It was so, no doubt, and he was glad. It seemed to him, as he looked round the rose garden, the trees and bushes just breaking into leaf, some a delicate, fairylike green, some brown, some almost red, that it was the most suitable place in the world in which to tell a love tale; and it would have been bven the refine- rient of cruelty—not in the least like Olive—for uinyone to have sent a 'lover there, where every.. thing spoke of how and 'life and mci,inc nria.

"These young birds crow very loudly," he said to himself, smiling still, "but their voices break as they grow older and they chant another note. Mr. Clifford is in love with Miss Olive Brandon, and thinks there is only one woman in the world in the eyes of every man. Well, well, he'll find out his mistake one of these days, but I don't know that he'll be the happier for it!"

CHAPTER XL

OLIVE.

Paul Clifford felt thoughtful as he went onward. The genial rays of the sun struck him full in the eyes, but as yet were not strong enough to make him blink. The birds had come down from the trees. Some were indulging in a breakfast of anything they could find; some, for want of a puddle or pool, were taking a dust bath. A lark that had risen early was splitting his little throat in triumphant song high in the heavens, where he looked like black, quivering speck. A rollicking, gambolling breeze played with the leaves and branches of the trees, bending them gracefully together while they exchanged confidences, flinging them backward inkearty laughter at the jokes they had imparted. A Weary dew like a curtain of gossamer rose from the lowlands, the hills were enveloped mist.

What a fool be had been ! Here was the prettiest, absolutely the prettiest girl he had ever seen, and he had offended her even before he had seen her!

"I should be only too delighted to stay on in this beautiful part of our snug little island—a part to which I am an absolute stranger," he answered, the smile on his face as he glanced at Jack the acme of insolence—"but dear old Jack seems inclined to wish me to postpone my visit until his return. But I shouldn't at all mind " —he laughed boyishly —"paying another visit then."

Paul Clifford made a step forward. It was natural, of course, that he should feel jealous. This man looked at the woman who was now his promised wife, with something more than admira- tion in his glance. Paul did not know all; did not understand, did not dream for etninute that he had been shut away for long, long months in a prison, where not even an unbeautiful, common woman was ever seen, where even in the infirmary warders, men nurses attended to the sick. Warrington Field Club

The botanical meeting of the Warrington Field Club, was held in the Museum Lecture Room, on Friday. Mr. T. Fairhurst presided over a good attend- ance. There were a large numbn of beautiful c-tcl interesting specimens exhibited by 'rations members and displayed on tables and st•mds. After the inspection of these, Mr. A. B. Kenwcn ,hy read a papec on Atropa belladonna," the deadly nightshade. dealing with the structure of root, st3m, leaf and flower, and illustrating the subje,st with diagrams and a series of microscopic sections. Womersley contribute 3 a psi sr on the flowers of South Wales, contrasting them, as observ: 1 by him during his residence in Monmouthshire, with the flora of the Warrington district. Mr. G. A. Dunlop exhibited a number of beautifully-mounted specimens, and gave notes on his resort's collecOng, dealing especially with the disklay of flowers and fruits, which had been so attractive a feature of the Museum and whose educational value and stimrlating effect on many of the rising generation have 1 sen largely due to Mr. Dunlop's skill and enthusiasm. Mr. L, Greening exhibited and spoke briefly on a fine series of mounted specimens illustrative of the dis- persal of seeds and the structural modifications by which this is effected. Mr. Madeley exhibited a manu- script prepared by Dr. Billups, a former member of the club. TFIE EXAMINER.

13110111518E1R 15th 1906.

The House of Lords is THE PEERS' thoroughly incorrigible LATEST after all. It appears to have BLUNDER, no sort of moral control over itself .For the facetious ness or the factiousness of its movements it

is like a wind blowing over the Broads or the deep sea. At the very end of its Wednesday's proceedings, when Lord LANSDOWNE had been exerting himself all the evening to suppress amend- ments to the Trades Disputes Bill, and when be had succeeded in every instance but one the Peers turned round in a sort of freak and ruined everything. They actually accepted at the instance of an Irish landlord, who is haunted by the spectres of Land Leagues a new clause which excludes the very greatest of our industries from the operation of the Bill. The terms of the Earl of DONOUGEMOILE'S new clause are to the effect that nothing in the Bill shall apply to anything done or contemplated in the furtherance of a dis- pute coming from or outside of, or directly or indirectly connected with, the use or occupa- tion or enjoyment by any person or persons of any land or any building or buildings thereon. This shuts off the whole race of agricultural labourers from the benfits of the Bill. It may also be made to apply, as the LORD CHANCELLOR pointed out, to disputes in the building trade, where the buildings concerned are of an agricultural character. Lord DONOUGHMORE spoke as if his new clause would affect Ireland only, and that was possibly his intention, but there is no mention of Ireland in his new clause, which is, without doubt, of general application. By accepting his proposal, the House of Lords has not only swept away, as with a broom, all the effects of its previous complacency with regard to the Trades Disputes Bill, but has enormously strengthened the indictment against its ways and its pretensions.

===== 0002601_19061215_art0094.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====

===== 0002601_19061215_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, December 15, 1906.

Our information is to the effect that the leaders of the Conservative party in the House o Commons were as much astonished by the actio of the House Of Lords as anybody else can possibly be. They were both astonished and indignant. Indeed the CHIEF WHIP is said to have gone to the length of using exceedingly direct and forcible language. And no wonder, for what is it that ha. 4 been upset besides the main purpose of the Bill, s ) far as it relates to such movements as that which was led by Mr. JOSEPH ARCH ? Nothing less than a scheme for dividing the Labour party and the Socialists from the general body of Liberal mem- bers in the House of Commons and from the general bo3y of the Nonconformists out of doors on the subject of the Education Bill. It was not a very deep or a very well-informed scheme, but it was a most important part of Mr. BALFOUR'S tactical arrangements, !supported in the House of Lords by Lord LANSDOWNE, who stamped out almost all the Lords' amendments; but unhappily for himself and his party, failed to stamp out this extraordinary new clause which, as an expression of enlightenment, is worthy of the Dark Ages. What Lord Do OUGIDIORE will, no doubt, regard as an excessively successful inter- vention on his own part adds the Trades Disputes Bill to the measures which will have to be sent back to the House of Lords for reconsideration. The Donofighmore amendment cannot, of course, be accepted in any form. It is capable of no satis- factory modification. It will simply have to be shorn away. The Lords might as well have taken it for granted that the Bill as it stood until Wednesday night was the minimum of what the Trade Unions will accept. It is strange that they should so insist on their own humiliation, which includes the humiliation of the Conservative leaders and involves great damage to the Con- servative cause.

In referring to the work of BEA.MONT SCHOOL the Education Committee B.EADMA.ETERSHIP. during the past year, at the prize distribution on Thurs- day night Alderman Bennett said that criticism was now almost silenced and there was" praise and laudation on every hand." We admit that the Committee has done its work amicably and well, but the enthusiastic chairman was a little wide of the mark in using the phrase we have just quoted. Instead of "praise and laudation" there is much gnashing of teeth at the present moment in local educational circles. Members of the teaching profession are in a fury— and not unreasonably—because the managers of the new Beattiont School have recommended an outsider for the post of headmaster at a salary of £25. The obvious conclusion is that these gentlemen do not consider there is a man among th 3 assistant head teachers of Warring- ton competent for the post. The teachers strongly dissent from such a conclusion and we agree with the protest they have raised. There I me plenty of men in our big elementary schools fully qualified by the best experience for the post, but apparently they have been ruled out by some red-tape regulation in respect of certificates or " parchment." This is the first of the new schools under the present authority, and it will be a very sad precedent if we have to go out of town for a headmaster. Fortunately the matter is not finally settled, and so far it has failed to receive the confirmation of the School Management Corn mittee. The whole question will come before the Education Committee on Monday night, and an illuminating discussion may be expected. Some very powerful arguments will need to be forth- coming to convince the public that the ranks of the teachers in Warrington contain no men qualified for and worthy of promotion to a headmastership.

In his admirable paper on THE CITY "The City Beautiful BEAUTIFUL. before the Literary and Philosophical Society on Monday evening, Canon Morley Stevenson painted a glowing picture of what the City of the future ought to be like. In the discussion which followed, Mr. A. J. Jolley said the worthy Canon had made some important omissions. He had not told them where " the unhappy traveller in search of alcoholic refreshment ' was going to obtain the Supply his nature craved for, or where such buildings as the prison, the workhouse and the asylum were going to be. Canon Stevenson treated the criticism in the jocular spirit in which it was intended but later he was constrained to admit that there was more seriousness in an error pointed out by Mr. W. H. Sherburn. The "City Beautiful" was to be designed on circular lin 33 with separate tracks for tram cars and motor cars, it evidently being the intention that the motorist should career along at will without damage to anyone but himself. Unfor- tunately, however,as Canon Stevenson had planned the city, the public would have to cross the motor track in order to get to the trains ! The Ipcturer smilingly promised that this mistake should be remedied in any future outline of the scheme.

Strong objection to the THE SUNDAY nature of the Sunday press PRESS, are finding expression in many quarters. It seems to have become the custom in one or two news- paper offices not one hundred miles from here to carefully collect all the filth available from the courts each week and serve it up spicily with big head.lines and imaginative sketches for Sunday consumption. Even more than the ordinary daily newspaper, the Sunday publications find their way into the home, for the simple reason that they cannot be delivered at the office or picked up at the bookstalls on the Sabbath. There is no more pernicious literature published openly in this country, and one paper. at least, with a large Sunday circulation in Lancashire and Cheshire, , is a disgrace to the honoured name of journalism. We were very glad to see that the Manchester District of the Institute of Journalists had con- demned this depraved type of journalism, and directed magisterial attention to the matter. The paper we have mostly in mind makes a speciality of divorce cases, criminal assault cases, and other stories from the police courts which ara never reported by the self-rospecting press. It is an unwritten newspaper law that certain of these cases shall not be reported. Yet here we see them specialised and recorded with an attention to detail, which is disgusting and demoralising. If there is not a section of the criminal law under which newspaper proprietors could be prosecuted for this kind of indecent exposure, it is a sad omission. In the public interest the police should endeavour to obtain a test case. This open parade of indecency on the Sabbath or any other day must be stopped.

Mr. James Williams, the CHESHIRE COUNTY Cheshire County finditOr, FINANCE, has just issued his abstract of accounts for the year ending March 31st last, and from it we glean some interesting particulars. Daring the year the sum of £572,210—including all rates—was received into the County Fund, and this with the cash balance of £39,227 at the beginning of the year, placed £611,437 at the disposal of the Council. The actual cash payments out of the ' County Fund have been £564,899, leaving a balance of £46,538 for liabilities. The Council received during the year the sum ot £12,325 from the Government under the " Agricultural Rates Act!" also, the sum of £1,061 was received on the Elementary Education Account. The pay. ment for maintenance of main roads (Rural and Urban) was £82,889, or £5,643 over the previous year's account. The General and Local Police Rates averaged 91-d. in the £. for the year. The authorized strength of the Police Force was 1 Chief Constable, 1 Deputy Chief Constable, 12 Superintendents, 19 Inspec- tors, 76 Sergeants, and 372 Constables.

With regard to Education matters, the numner of schools financed during the year was 343 (300 provided and 43 non-provided); the day school teachers numbered 2,039. Receipts and pay- ments on higher education show that the Com- mittee had £84,043 at their disposal, of which they expended £66,482. For elementary education there was a sum of £204,756, of which .f.,183,654 was expended in salaries, equipment, loans, main- tenance in industrial schools, &c. The county rate assessment for the assessable value on 31st March, 1900, was £3,503,991 ; and a rate at a penny in the 2 amounted to £14,600.

Lord Wolseley's outburst LORD WOLSELEY'S of indignation because his "SCARE." countrymen have not suffi- -'731 *VS dent fear of invasion in their hearts is interestingly reminiscent of his opposition to the channel tunnel scheme. Bat, after all, with whatever scorn Lord Worseley may regard politicians in general, he can hardly deny that the attitude which has been adopted towards the problem of national and Imperial defence has been taken with the approval of may student of military and political conditions whose word would be heard with respect in the most exclusive mess. The public must remember, to), that, with all these sneers at "jawers and talkers," the politician with a responsible post has a wider view than the soldier, of all the constituent facts which go to make up the problem to be faced. Lord Wolseley, regards the Admiralty as grabbing everything for the Navy, which is rather a pity, as it lays him open to the charge of jealousy. The main point of his letters to Lord Wemyss is however, his own fear that England is actually open to invasion from the Continent and that in order to safeguard ourselves we must have a strong Army. With that there is no real ground of quarrel. But a strong Army is not necessarily a large Army, and the present directors of our military forces are trying to do what Lord Wolseley did not succeed in doing—to make the Army strong by increased efficiency. He may rest assured that, until the country sees it is getting return for the money it is now spending on its land forces, it will not agree to any further sub- stantial increase.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Post early and often !—is a fair summary of the Postmaster's instructions for the Christmas season.

* * The " City Beautiful " has a good deal to contend with in the " Slum Injurious,' and so far effort on the most practical lines is not very evident. Like the poor, the slums are always with us and seem likely to remain.

* * * "Do you realise that if Mary Ann, in letting her young man out after feeding him with your supper, should under the natural emotion of parting, fall and hurt herself, or dislocate her ankle, or in anyway injure herself, she will have a claim against you for ' damages" ?—" World." ill 0 The Postmaster again wishes our readers to post early in order to reduce the congestion at Christmas. Letters for delivery on Christmas Day will be accepted at the various offices in the District from December 13th to December 22 inclusive. The town letter boxes will be cleared for the night mail at 8 p.m. on the 24th inst., and at 10.30 p.m. at the General Post Office. .

* The trust system is run to such an extent in America that it even controls the professional boxing. In California they have what is called the Fight Trust, which puts up different men to do battle in the ring, and doubtless makes a big profit judging by the interest which is taken in prize fighting over there. Thousand of tickets are sold and special trains run for some of the big contests. * * * How rapidly the movement for beautifying our industrial centres is gaining ground is evidenced by the fact that Canon Stevenson, who .4?as done such splendid. work in this direction in Warrington, has already received many invitations to speak on the subject at other large centres. He has already addressed a meeting at Leeds, and other places which he has been asked to visit include citiea so far distant from each other as Liverpool and Bristol. * * * Alderman Bennett is not the man to be daunted by opposition or interruption. He has spoken under many and varying conditions in his time, but on Thursday he had a new experience, his speech in reply to a vote of thanks being delivered to the accompaniment of aggressive " cyphering " from the organ, which refused to be silenced. The Alderman, however, stuck manfully to his task, although the majority of the audience were quite unable to hear his remarks.

Facts are more likely to influence the gambler than the most eloquent and logical appeals. And fortunately facts are easily found which prove the rank folly of betting. A writer in " Tit-Bits ' put an imaginary sovereign on every horse recommended by ten sporting papers for big races last week. On just one day his gains exceeded his losses, and at the end of the week he had lost £.52 18s. Gcl. Only one of the papers found ten winners; while for eighteen out of the thirty-seven races the winners were not given by a single one of the ten tipsters.*

Alderman Bennett has hitherto enjoyed the distinc- tion of being regarded, as our local dreamer, but now a rival has appeared in the person of the Re*. C. Harvey Cook. Like the alderman, the minister at Cairo-street is a very enthusiastic supporter of the movement for making Warrington beautiful and he has been conjuring up picturesque visions of the Warrington of the future. His pet dream, however, is of the river banks at Axpley beautifully laid out,— shady trees, seats &c.,—with steps leading down to the river 'on which pleasure boats will ply. 'Adjoin- ling the banks playing fields would be arranged in the most alluring way, and the townspeople would be able to dispost themselves at leisure. The picture is a pretty one. Let us hope it may some day be realised. A A

The Cheshire County Council Education Committee has made an important departure in the way of pro- viding facilities for secondary education. Regulations have been adopted for the paymont of traelling expenses for scholars living more than three miles from a secondary school, including railway fares where necessary, so long as the cost does not exceed .£1 per year. This is a distinct step forward and proves the Cheshire Committee to be working with a single aim for the advancement of the youth of the county. At first blush it seems somewhat like a relief of parental responsibibilitv, but it is vastly more legitimate and reasonable than the provision of free breakfasts for elementary scholars. Further parti- culars respecting the regulations may be obtained from Mr. R. P. Ward, Director oVEdacation, Chester. ** * , It is getting time an exclusive tariff was drawn up for the voluntary occupants of His Majesty's gaols, or they will be full of suffragettes. Five more were incarcerated yesterday in preference to paying the usual fine for disorderliness. A little variety was given to the proceedings by the presence among the defendants of a real live male, who had also been shouting" votes for women" and had tried to trip up a constable. The young man's father said'• he bore an irreproachable character—he did not drink, he did not smoke, and was a vegetarian," yet he "wanted to Igo to prisdn." This youthful paragon was accordingly accomodated by the magistrates with the usual 20s. or 14 days. The fate he feared most was to be sent home without the option. He ought to have been smacked ! .. .

Sir Henry F. Hibbert is an able and attractive spermer and his address at the Parr Hill on Thursday evening was of a practical c haracter. One great weakness in our educational system to which he called serious attention was the long break which oftener than not occurs between a boy's leaving school at a very early age and his resumption of study. In this way a great deal of ground is lost and, as Sir Henry pointed out. there is considerable waste of public money. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

ESTATE AGENT'S BANKRUPTCY

At the Warrington Bankruptcy Court on Friday before Mr. Registrar Ridgwayy John Henry Harrison,estate agent Waterloo-road, Runcorn, appeared for his public exam- ination. Debtors statement of affairs disclosed liabilities of £2,325 Gs. 3d., and assets £9O 10s. 11d, with a deficiency of £2,231 15s. 4d.—After s few preliminary questions, Mr. Johnson, official receiver, asked debtor if he had not been living on his creditors for a considerabie , number of years.—Harrison : That is the only way to put it I suppose. Continuing, debtor said he attributed his failure to bad debts and declining business. He had carried on business in an insolvent condition for 20 years. Three days before he filed his petition he borrowed £35 to pay money which he had collected in the shape of rents.—A creditor: What was the £lOO for which you borrowed from me?—For payment of rents— Then your statement that you required it for the extension of your business was not correct ?—lf I made that statement it was not correct.—When in March of this year I agreed to become guarantor for you at the bank 1 for £7O, you overdrew to the extent of £l9 Sc. 7d. did, and no one regrets more than I do that I did.—You represented to me at the time that you were worth £l,OOO ?—lmpossible. Continuing, debtor said at the end of several quarters he had to borrow money to pay in rents which he had collected.—The creditor : I suggest that when I lent you money I was induced to do so owing to misrepresentation ?—According to you, yes; according to me, no.—The examination was closed.

RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday, when there were present :—Messrs. G. Pilling (in the chair), T. Baxter, S. Woodward, G. Egerton-Warburton, T. Wright, C. E. Linaker, C. E. Linaker, jun., J. Spruce, J. H. Davies, H. Eyes. J. Whitley. H. Mawdsley, C. Mounfield, T. Antwis, W. Harrison, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), J. Swinton and \V. Diggle (surveyors). J. Farringtou (inspector), and Dr. Adams (medical officer). STREET LIGHTING AT STOCKING HEATH. The Engineer to the Warrington Corporation Gas ' Works writing under date of the 6th inst.with reference to the Stockton Heath lighting said the price of gas was fixed by their Act of Parliament and they had made no departure from the same. Gas lamps were lighted 3,tho hours per annum. He could not recommend the Cor- poration to consent to experimenting with meters unless they (the Rural Council) decided to undertake the lighting themselves. In a further letter on the Sth inst the Engineer said : We regret the lamps have gone out. All the lamps on the Ist, 15th, 16th and 21st November were stopped with napthalene ; on the 12th and 19th the lamp-lighter was late owing to the very rough night. The great expense to which we are put by the napthalene stopping thffpipes would be prevented if we knew a remedy. The lighting in Stockton Heath is performed with as much care as that for the town and we are quite unable to do anything more for you, The Clerk read the following copy and resolution passed by the Warrington Town Council. "That this committee are of opinion that there is no real ground for complaint, but in order to give the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee an opportunity of carrying out the lighting more effectively if they can this committee will be prepared to supply gas in bulk at the usual price and hand over the lighting and care of the lamps to that committee on terms to be arranged." Mr. Whitley said he did not think the Corporation replies were satisfactory. The Corporation had taken off the burners of the lamps and substituted smaller burners, which crystalized. It was necessary that they should have their streets well lighted so that the aged and infirm in particular would not run into any obstruc- tions. (Laughter and a voice "Trees. The matter was referred to the Stockton Heath Lighting Committee.

ST HELENS.

BRUTAL ST. HELENS HUSBAND. James Orford, collier, of 28, Boundary-road, St. Helens, who had been locked up for Pullwfully wound- ing his wife Kate, was dealt with by the magistrates on Monday. The evidence was that the man had been drinking at the week end, and had refused to give his wife any money. They had been previously separated, and on Saturday afternoon heknocked her down and hit her on the head, inflicting a serious wound. The charge was reduced to one of aggravated assault, and Orford was flood 40s. and costs, and the wife granted a separation, with an order for Is. 6[l. per week. ST. HELENS AND IHE CONGO ATROCITIES. The Mayor of St. Helens (Alderman C. J. Bishop) on Monday presided at a meeting held in his com- mittee-room at tho Town Hall, when a resolution was passed on the subject of the Congo atrocities.—The Rev. J. H. Harris, of the Congo Reform Association, gave a lengthy address on the position of affairs, and the steps that had been taken to effect a remedy for the shocking manner in which the natives were treated.— The Vicar of St. Helens (the Rev. Cyril Bardsley) moved :—" That this public meeting of St. Helens citizens desires to press upon his Majesty's Government , the nedtl for energetic action to secure for the natives of the Ccmgo Free State protection from outrage and bad government, and pledges itself to support Fs Majesty's Ministers in any action they may take for that purpose."—Mr. W. Gamble seconded, and the Rev. D. Jones supported.—The resolution was unanimously carried, and, in concluding the meeting, the Mayor cordially thanked the Rev. W. Holroyd for the trouble he had taken in arranging the meeting.

ST. HELEN'S LABOURER'S- THEFTS. John Jackson, labourer, of 7, Ravenhea,d-passage, was charged at St. Helens on Wednesday with stealing three lots of lead from Messrs. Nuttall and Co.'s bottle works. Suspicion was aroused, mid cn Tuesday he was seen to take the lead and leave the works, and the lead was found. secreted round his waist. The bench said .it was a very serious matter, but as it was prisoner's first offence for stealing he would be fined £2, or twenty- eight days on the one charge, the others being with drawn.

PO LICE.

At Newton-le-Willows Police Court on Thursday, John T. C. Ashurst, traveller for beer riud stout for Messrs. E. Dickinson and Co., 101, Darlington-street, Wigan, was fined 2ili. and costs for persouating the police at EarlestoNvu on November 22. It appeared from the evidence that defendant went to Mr. M. Seton, a local bookmaker, describing himself as a detective in the county pollee, Wigan, alleged that Mr. Salon's clerk had assaulted him, for which he was to be reported, and then asked Seton to have a quiet walk in order to reason together, as it would pay him better. Defend- ant threatened for two pins to arrest another man, and told Frank Dale, another bookmaker, that he had better mind or he would have him locked up. Defendant denied Seton's allegations. SEQUEL TO A BURNING FATALITY. COUNTY COURT ACTION. . us At the Warrinston County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Deputy Judge Strachan, K.C., and a jury, Frederick Fox, 22, Hotel street, Earlestown, brought an action against Samuel Harvey, printer and stationer, Bridge-street, Earlestrea a, to recover 211 for losses sustained by the death of his daughter Norah Fox, who was burnt to death on June 18th. Mr. C. H. Unsworth appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. A. Browne for the defendant. Mr. Unsworth said the claim was for damages for the I expense which plaintiff had been put to owing to the alleged negligence of the defendant's servant, which they said was the consequent cause of the death of plaintiff's daughter North, 41 years of age. It appeared that in June the defendant's son instructed a boy named Phillips to take two bags of waste paper on to some land and burn it. The boy set fire to the paper and then went away to attend to some other busines. Unfortunately the plaintiff's child wandered on this waste land, and it being a rather windy day it was sur- mised that the child's clothing caught fire, and the died at the Infirmary two days later.
