WARRINGTON.

Bible Class Social. The seventh annual social in connection with St Paul's Men's Bible Class was held on Tuesday even- ing in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street. Commencing at eight o'clock the proceeding, were continued until midnight, and during that time the 50 couples present engaged in dancing (to music supplied by Mr. Albert Trow), games and in listening to an admirable programlne of vocal music,the artistes being Miss Armstrong, Mr. and Mrs. Hannan, and Mr. R. Bailey. The arrangements reflected considerable credit on the committee of which Mr. A. Binks acted as chairman, Mr. H. Broadhurst as hon. treasurer, and Mr. Joseph Robinson as hon. secretary. During an interval in the programme, the Vicar delivered a short address in which he referred to the success of the Bible Class andvrged the members to attend it regularly. fie thanlked Mr. Binks, Mr. Broadhurst and Mr. Robinson for their valuable services. Warrington Photographic Society. _ _ _

Mr. Henry Bond was the lecture; at the usual meeting of the above Society on Tuesday, tvhen he demonstrated to the members his method of enlarging on Barnet bromide paper. The whole operations, which were carried out with the ease of a practised 'Worker, were followed with interest and his remarks on and illustration of how to maniputate the exposure for effects of atmosphere, &c., cannot fail to be of value to workers of bromide paper. A hearty round of applause signified the satisfaction of the company for an instructive and interesting demonstration. —At a special meeting of the society on Wednesday Mr. C. H. L. Jackson of London gave a practical demonstration on the making of enlarged negatives on Rotograph paper. Notwithstanding the vagaries of the weather there was a good number present and the various points raised by the demonstrator in favour of paper negatives were fully appreciated by them. Mr. Mathias presided and an instructive evening was spent. Royal London Friendly Society A function of a very interesting character was held on Wednesday evening last at the Winmarlegh Caf , Sankey-street, when Councillor Ashcroft, the respected uperintendent of the Warrington Branch of the Royal London Friendly Society, extended a very cordial invitation to the whole of the members of his staff and their lady friends, to the number of sixty- eight, to a social and convivial evening. The sumptuous repast was, needless to say, done full justice to. After tea the party settlee themselves down to a musical evening, Mr. Ashcroft presiding. During an interval opportunity was taken of making a presentation to Mr. Ashcroft, to celebrate his com- pletion of twenty-five years as Superintendent of the Royal London Friendly Society (Warrington Branch). Collector J. Foden made the presentation of a hand- some illuminated address which had been contributed to by every member of the staff. The recipient feel- ingly replied and said that nothing on his part would be wanting to ensure the good feeling which had always existed between the staff and himself. Mrs. Ashcroft was next made the recipient of a beautiful silver rose bowl, which was supported on an ebony plinth and which bore a suitable inscription. Later in the evening the party adjourned to the ballroom, where the concluding hour was spent• in dancing. Children's League of Pity Meeting. One of the most important of the charitable institutions of the country jethe National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, which, in the great industrial centres in particular, is doing incal- culable good., The Children's League of Pity is a powerful factor itt raising the funds with which to carry on the work, and in years past the Warrington Bratich has been held up as an example to others for activity and strength. An effort is being mlde to again claim this distinction and judging by the number of new members enrolled at a meeting on Wednesday, the endeavour will probably he crowned with success. The meet- ing was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe and was well attended. An interesting addres was delivered by tfrs. Frank Bush. People, she said, seemed to think that the object of the N.S.p.C.C. was to remove ill-treated children from their homes and punish the parents. That was not so, their object being, as the title of the Society indicated, to prevent cruelty. Mrs. Bush proceeded to emphasise the need for the operations of the Society by a number of pathetic cases dealt with by the Society.—At the close of the address Miss Broadbent, hon. sec. of the War- rington Branch, was heartily thanked for her services during the year, a similar compliment being paid to Mrs. °Bush for her address. Refreshments were provided.

Christmas Excursions. The list of excursions arranged by the London and North-Western Railway Company for the Christmas and New Year holidays is very extensive, numerous day and period trips having been fixed to run from their Warrington (Bank Quay) station, and to some of the most important of these we would draw the attention of our readers. To those wishing to spend the holidays across the border, excursions are scheduled to run to Carlisle, Edinboro' and Glasgow by the West Coast route, on Friday night December 21st., Monday night Deceinbtr 24th,4snd Friday night December 28th, in connection with which, cheap bookings will be given to the principal stations in Scotland. In addition, a through special for Carlisle, Edinboro' and Glasgow only will be run on New Tear's eve. A special excursion for 5 days will be run on Saturday December 22nd to York,Newcastle on- Tyne,and other stations in the North Eastern District. There will be through excursions to London (Euston) on Christmas night December 25th, Saturday, December 29th, and Monday December 31st, in connection. with which, cheap tickets will be issued to the Continent. To South and Central Wales on December 22nd, by the short and direct route via the Central Wales line. Excellent facilities are also given to those desirous of visiting Ireland, the Company having arranged cheap trips, by their well known and popular routes via Fleetwood and Holyhead on December 21 and 24. In addition, cheap tickets will be issued to Liverpool, Manchester, &c. Full information, times and fares may be obtained on application for bills at the station. Messrs. Dean and Dawson have made admirable arrangemepts for Christmas and New Year's holiday excursions by the Great Central Railway. sf,3cial attention is called to the running of the non-stop, through and restaurant car express to Bristol, Exeter, Plymouth, and West of England, on Saturday, December 22nd, leaving Warrington at 4,25 p.m. There will also be excursions to, York and North- Eastern stations on December 22nd and to London (Marylebone) on Wednesday, December 96th, Satur- day, December 29th, and Monday, December 31st. An excursion will run to Goole and Bull on Wednesday, Pecember 26th.

DEAR RELAfIONS,— By the time this letter is in front ef you, we shall be well on our way towards the festive season, the season of good-will and merriment, and the season when we shall com- memorate old friendships and make new. As far as making new goes, I must say that I am extremely fortunate in every season and in every week. My letters this week contain five applications for membership, and what do you all think (please prepare yourself for something marvellous)? Why, one of the new members is a nephew—there now The first new nephew for two or three weeks. Well, we shall have to look to him to .make up for all the others by sending us some, interesting little tit-bits. I have received an exceedingly nice warm- looking 'woollen petticoat from our niece Lucy Fitzpatrick, to whom I am awarding the prize for the best weekly article sent in, whilst the usual story prizes are this week shared by Doris Bond and Arthur Fearn. You must excuse the shortness of my letter this week, as I must really begin to save up one ot two good things for the Christmas one.

UNCLE NED.

NURSERY RHYMES. A was an Archer, who shot at a frog, B was a Butcher, who kept a bull-dog. C was a Captain, all covered with lace„ D was a Drummer, who played with much grace. E was an Esquire, with pride on his brow, F was a Farmer, who followed the plough. G was a Gamester, who had but ill-luck, H was a Hunter, who hunted a buck. I was an Italian, who had a white imouse, J was a Joiner, who built up a house. K was a King, so mighty and grand,' L was a Lady, who had a white hand. M was a Miser, who hoarded up gold, vN was a Nobleman, gallant and bold. O was an Organ-boy, who played about town, P was a Parson, who wore a black gown. Q was a Queen, who was fond of her people, I: was a Robin, who perched on a steeple. S was a Sailor, who spent all he got, T *as a Tinker, who mended a pot. II *as an Usher, who loved little boys, ✓ was a Veteran, who sold pretty toys. W was a Watchman, who guarded the door, X was eXpensive, and so became poor. Y was a Youth, who did not love school, Z was a Zany, who looked a great fool.

A CLEVER FOX.

Here is a short story of a. cute fox which I heard not long ago:—

"Hard pressed by the hounds, he crossed an open field, only to find his way barred by a high wire-netted fence, which he could not squeeze through. " Three times he tried to clear in a jump, and three times he fell back. "%Thal was to be done? The hounds were coming pelting across the field. _

" Did Reynard know the proverb, 'The boldest course is often the safest?' He paused a moment to think, then he turned and bolted straight into -the oncoming pack! The leading hounds, with their noses tp the trail, did not see him, and the others never attempted to stop liim. A good many of them stared at him suspiciously, wonderingly, but highly trained dogs are something like highly trained soldiers. They await the word of command, and are quick to act on it when they get it, but if it dog not come they don't act at all. _ "So, as the leaders gave no sign, the other hounds simply followed on, and let the fox go by. But I fancy % the more clever, ones must have felt that 'there was something wrong somewhere.' How Reynard must have winked as he slipped safely through, and got clear away." E. H.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am sending you a comical piece of poetry which I hope you will be pleased with. It is entitled:—

THE MARAUDERS.

Our cookie was a careful maid, Who loved her work right well: And oft there issued from her realm

An appetising smell. Now in the yard there lived three dogs,

. Good friends were all the three: - And much they wished to be good friends With cook, if it might be. But (!.f their friendship cook had none,.

She drove them from her door; She scarce would toss them e'en a bone

From out her goodly store. And so, a sort of warfare grew, Where friendship might have been; And daring raids were made when cook

Was nowhere to be seen. And once it happ'd when Christmas time Was calling to the cooks, A. monster pudding cookie made, The best in all the books. She stirred it well, she boiled it well;

- And proudly hung it by, Outside the door! this wonder then The dogs did quickly spy.

Two dozen old fellows, some Shorter than others, Lived two thousand years, And all of them brothers. Notwithstanding their ages, They're as upright as ever; And none will deny But they're all very clever. They are the admiration Of great and small, And have got but one eye Amongst them all. Dear Uncle Ned,—l send you two little con- tributions, one a piece of poetry, and the other a short story.

THE REVENGEFUL MAUt

A German farmer had a tame stork, and one day he brought home a young wild one to be a companion for it. But the tame stork was very angry, and set upon the stranger and pecked and beat it with its wings so mercilessly that the poor bird could hardly manage to save itself by limping off. Some weeks later the strange stork returned, but this time not alone. He • was accompanied by three other storks, and all four of them set upon the tame stork, pecking it so badly that it would have died if its master had not arrived just in time to save it.

I remain, your loving niece, Doan BOND.

A NIGHT WITH THE FAIRIES.

Once upon a time in a cosy room sat a boy and girl. By their tones and looks they were evidently quarrelling. But what about t Can you guess? Well, it was about fairies. The boy abused her beloved fairies, and said he did not believe in them. The same evening Jack had to go along a dark and lonely road. "Look out for the fairies," whispered Ivy before he went, but he took no notice of her. When returning, it was dark, and Jack felt a bit frightened. Suddenly funny little beings darted out of the darkness and put a bandage on his eyes and carried him away. When his bandage was removed, a blaze of light struck his eyes. Looking around him he found him- self in a rot house with no top to it. Pretty fairy lights all colours were hanging about, and he was isurrounded by pretty little things with wings dressed in different dblours, who, he thought, must be Ivy's fairies. He was brought before the throne of the king and queen, who asked who he was. -."He is the . brother of your .most faithful subject, Ivy Gray," they replied. He was ordered to be given a bed and food. Then he was borne away once more and given a plate of fairy fool, and after he had eaten it he felt more hun- gry than before. They took him to a little room, where he undressed and got into a green velvet bed with yellow dots. After they had sung him to sleep they carried away his clothes. In the morning he awoke shivering with cold, and a pricking sensation all over. Sitting up, he looked around and found, with terror, he was lying on a furze bush in bloom, a lot of short pigmy trees around him, and his clothes had quite disappeared. As it was early and nobody about he slipped.home and got into bed. The next morning Iv'y asked him with a smile. whether he believed in fairies. And he ad- mitted he did.

NELLIE VOUND.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR

, NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good feth.

Pauper Labour. Sir,—Will you please let me relate a little incident. Some 20 years ago I was Hon. Secretary of the North St. Pancras (London) Ratepayers' Association, when a Conservative friend of mine, a member of the same Association and also on the St. Pancras Board of Guar- dians, brought before my notice the following fact : A carpenter and joiner by a long and successive series of illness and misfortune, commencing with an attack of rheumatic fever, found himcelf in the Workhouse Infirmary, and his wife and little children in the House itself,—ablack and dismal prospect. Thanks, however. to the care and attention given to him, when transferred from the Inlirmary to the House, with increasing strength came confidence, inspiring hope. If he only had another chance, he could again take his plate on terms of equality amongst his fellow men ; have his own little home with his wife by his side, supporting his children and trying to guide their future himself. But how was it to be done? He had nothing. He applied to the Board of Guardians' said if he only had £5 as a loan for a short time he thought he could accomplish this, and relieve the ratepayers of the burden of his family. But the Guardians had no power or authority to lend money, and especially upon such slender security as this man's word. And it was at this point I war. made acquainted with the story. A littlel investigation proved the man's character and integrity as a workman and citizen had le: en exceptionally good. A few friends found the money, handed it to him, with- out any written or other security but his word, and he got his chance. He got work immediately with an old employer. In a very Mica,: time he had his wife and children with him in their own little home, which he did not wait too long to embellish with very luxurious furniture, or to find out how very much more he wanted, before he came to repay the whole of the money lent him, and which he was told was now a free gift and might be used for the comfort of his family. This created a very strong impri .sion in my mind, and my reason for relating it is, that it so forcibly emphasized the impossibility of the • man shaking off the fettera of pauperism without the aid of ontside,private,or semi-charitable effort. The use this man made of his opportunity shines very much in contrast to that of the individual around whom this criticism has encircled, who failed the very first week of his emancipation to meet his obligation to his own daughter who so kindly helped him. it was the spirit of helping, the responding to the solicitation that I understood my friend Mr. Osboi oe to depix Ate, or depreciate, and it seemed so foreign to my conception of him. The exploitation of man by man,is just now quite a second- ary or entirely separate question to the one before Us, of how is the pauper to get out of the Workhouse and take his place again in the ranks of labour unless assisted by outside help, which may be charity of thought, charity of purse or a little of both. Were I a drowning man I should not quarrel with the thickness of the rope thrown to me, so long as it was sufficiently strong enough to bring me safely to the shore. There- fore T think that dwelling upon the suggested injustice to this individual one ia apt to lose sight of the spirit that prompts to help. I know Mr. Osborne will accert my statement that I know nothing more of this case that I have read in the columns of the "Examiner." He is quite right when he says that unskilled merely preceded the skilled labour. So do rises in pay fro, quently follow, and there is nothing to show it would not in this case. At the outset his quibbling gave him a penny an hour more, and with a week's wages in his pocket, he was in a better position to make his further demands than when he started, but he evidently had not got the grit of the great majority of our working men; he did not last long enough to solve that problem. I shall not recapitulate the numerous evils my friend Mr. Osboine points out, and asks me cannot I see them. I c m neither vouch for nor contradict his figures, but I accept the facts. Let me just add, at the period I men- tioned commencing this letter or a little before it I was also presiding at large public meetings in St. Pancras in support of Mrs. Annie Hicks, the then Socialist caadidate for the• London School Board, one of the most prominent points in her programme being free meals for the poor and destitute children forced to go hungry to school, she being also supported by pen and public speech by John (now the Right Hon. John) Burns, H. Champion, Miss Helen Taylor, Mr. Hyndman, and other then prominent members of the Socialistic party. It was not then quite so acceptable a doctrine, the feeding of hungry children, as it appears to be to-day, and although ray activity has diminished I am just as keenly interested in all these social ques- tions P 9 ever I was. At that time I was a loyal Liberal; to-day I hope I am the same. believing that within its ranks exist the possibilities of remedying those problems which Mr. Osborne mentioos and very many o..her evils. I have to thank Mr. Osborne most heartily for his courtesy in dealing with my remarks.l feel there may be a distinction but very little difference in our views on many of these social problems. They are worth thinking about even if we do look at them from different positions, and I have to apologize to you and regret trespassing so much upon your space.—Believe me yours very truly, PATRICK O'BRIEN. 125, Wilderspool-road. October 11th, 1906. The Homelessness of the Poor. Sir,—The great Evangelist, D. L. Moody, never spoke a truer word then when he said, " You in Britain stand more in need of homes than you do of Churches." --The home was founded tefore the Church, and will outlast it. How strange it is that in England, which used to be famous for its home life (and perhaps is still famous amongst the favourably situated) we should find in every town, certainly in Warrington, slum houses which suggest that all that gives sweetness, beauty, and security to the term " Home Sweet Home" has gone. Even the Irish tenant with his mud floored cottage, and thatched roof, on the edge of a peat bog, has more of the feeling of home than the same Irishman, when driven by poverty he comes to our tea a to work as a labourer in a Warrington forge and is housed in a slum. He settles in a dismal street or more dismal yard from which his door opens directly to his living room. The house is one already condemned or ought to be. Every spot of surrounding earth is covered over with flags, setts, or cinder.; ; not a square yard at the back in which he can grow even a poi.ato, or a foot in front in which he can plant a geranium. The floor cf his children's playgrcund is the paved street, the gutters are the water courses, the blue sky above too often blotted out by a canopy of am eke. If the sunlight cau by dodging surrounding smoke and buildings look in at that " Home," it is unwelcome, for it but reveals its dull- ness and ugliness. The idea conveyed in the term of the "Homes of Merrie England" is to very many an echo of a far off age. Home should be the Englishman's castle, it is the keystone of the nation. It ought to be the cradle of an Imperial race. A nation's wealth is not in its posseesions or its gold, but in its men stalwart, sober, and virtuous, like the yeomanry of old ; such cannot be raised in slums. Shame upon us, if as a result of bad laws, and worse indiffer- ence, we have allowed conditions to grow up in our towns which degrade rather than uplift. What else can we expect from the environment we have allowed to gather rolind the home, the seed bed of the people? "The homeleasness of the masses" is not a mere figure of speech; it is an ugly naked truth,a deep seated canker destroying the nation's vitality. What interest in national life, what stake in his., country, has the slum dweller? We must give him some visible spot to which his humanity can cling, and from which his citizenship and patriotism can take their rise. It would be most unjust to class all who live in poor houses as either drunken, or indifferent to social and national ideals, but every social reformer, every politician, be he Consera- tive, Liberal, Labour or Socialist, in urging his ideals comes against a great mass of iudifferentisin ; an indifferentism, so pronounced that hundreds of men will sell their birthright of the vote for a pot of beer, and hundreds and thousands of women will sell their influ- ence for actin of tea or a ticket for coal. Such are a danger to the commonwealth and commonweal. What?,l is to be done with these imperfect, undeveloped lives e lam prepared to be told that the fault is in the pe'cipl, themselves, that reformation must start from within that they must give up their drunkennesa.must get their hearts changed S.m.,,itc. I know all this. Ido not under- value individual reformation, but recognise its impor- tance. But our poor must he given a fair chance. Society must ask li celf how far the destruction of home 'life, individul character, and will power. is caused by Iputting property rights before human rights, and how far excessive drinking is a result rather than a cause. The seed falling amongst thorns was a good as that which fell on the good ground, but the thorns choked it in 'spite of its vitality, and there was no harvest. The late Dean of Manchester realized the effect of environment when heconfessed that if he lived in the slums he would probably take to drink. There is in all lives a love of variety, an active restlesssness which craves expression, and this iS all the more intense if confined during the day by monotonous work. The middle class, the upper working classes (although these too have the faults of their class) can find their I recreation; interest, and excitement,in politics religion, music, theatres, etc., and out-door summer pursuitslike bowls, cycling, and gardening, but the slum dweller has only the public house ; it is his parlour, his club, his concert room—glad very often his ruin Some of us were trained in our youth to look upon the signing of the pledge as sufficient for Vie indi- vidual, and prohibition for the nation as the goal of the Statesman. More mature study of the question appears to me to reveal that the problem of intemperance is far more complex than it appeared to our youthful vision. We find that the question does not stand alone, but is interlocked with many other social difficulties of which the housing question is one of the Most impor- tant, and in speaking of the housing question I do not alone mean the structure of the house, but the whole environment which gathers round the word home. Upon these classes that are comfortably housed and homed, does not the obligation rest to help those who are in this particular unable to help themselves, to abolish slum property, and slum environments, and by well con- sidered action create sqcla conditions of life which will give the good that is in every human soul a fair field to develop. CETARLE s E. PARKER. Peaketh. December 12th, 1.9011.

WARRINGTON.

Licensing Applications.

At the Borough Police Court, on Monday, Mr. A. H. Clive, secretary of Atkinson's Cafes, Limited, applied for a music and dancing licence for Atkinson's Cafe, in Bridge-street. The licence was granted.—Mr. Garnett, architect, Sankey-street, applied for per- mission to make certain structural alterations at the Norton Arms Hotel. This application was also granted. Band of Hope Concert. _ _

The members of the Buckley-street Sunday school Band of Hope held an entertainment on Monday night in the schools. There was a good attendance, Mr. A. Jennings occupying the chair. Recitations were contributed by Miss 'L. Elwell, Master Abraham Makin, Miss E. Vale and Miss M. E. Makin, and a reading by Miss L. Buckley. Solos by Misses Nellie Lomax and Jane Harrison, and a quartette by Misses A. Leech and H. Kindon and Masters P. Kean and Harry Woolley. Bible Class Social.

The seventh annual social in connection with St Paul's Men's Bible Class was held on Tuesday even- ing in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street. Commencing at eight o'clock the procer.ding!l were continued until midnight, and during that time the 50 couples present engaged in dancing (to music supplied by Mr. Albert Trow), games and in listening r to an admirable programme of vocal music,the artistes being Miss Armstrong, Mr. and Mrs. Harman, and Mr. R. Bailey. The arrangements reflected considerable credit on the committee of which Mr. A. Binks acted as chairman, Mr. H. Broadhurst as hon. treasurer, and Mr. Joseph Robinson as ,hon. secretary. During an 1 interval in the programme, the Vicar delivered a short address in which be referred to the success of the Bible Class and urged the members to attend it regularly. He thanked Mr. Binks, Mr. Broadhurst and Mr. Robinson for their valuable services. Children's League of Pity Meeting. One of the most important of the charitable institutions of the country is the National Society for the Prevention of CI uelty .to Children, which, in the great industrial centres in particular, is doing meal. culable good. The Children's League of Pity is a powerful factor in raising the funds with which to carry on the work, and in years past the Warring Lem Branch has been held up as an example to others for activity and strength. An effort is being. made to again claim this distinction, and Judging by the number of new members enrolled at a meeting on Wednesday, the endeavour will probably be crowned with success. The meet- ing was held at the Winniarleigh Cafe and was well attended. An • interesting addres IVA 9 delivered by Urs. Frank Bush. People, she said, seemed to think that the object of the N.S.P.C.C. was to remove ill-treated children from their homes and punish the parents. That was not so, their object being, as the title of the Society indicated, to prevent cruelty. Mrs. Bush proceeded to emphasise the need for the operations of the Society by a number of pathetic eases dealt with by the Society. —At the close of the address Miss Broadbent, hon. sec. of the War- rington Branch, was heartily thanked for her services during the year, a simicar compliment being paid to Mrs. Bush for her address. Refreshments were provided.

Volunteer War Game. A "war-game " was played at the Volunteer head- quarters on Friday between the officers of the War- rington Corps and the officers of the Bth (Scottish) Volunteer Battalion tiyerp ml Regiment. Colonel Marshall West (the expert umpire for the "war games" of the Home District Tactical Society) came from London to act as umpire-in-chief. The opposing forces bad as commanders the colonels of the two battalions —Colonel A. L. Mae' and Lientenant-dolonel j. A. Edelsten. Major C. S. Simpson (Adjutant to the Liverpool Scottish) and Captain Gordon Roberts acted as assistant umpires. The majors and most oZ the officers of the Warrington corps took part. The terrain of the encounter was the country lying around the river Thames between Wallingford and Henley. The "Red" or Warrington force were a portion of the Home Army, the main body of which had suffered a severe defeat at the hands of a " Blue " force of invaders, represented by the Liverpool Corps. "Reds' " problem in this exercise was to keep" Blue" at bay, while the " Red " removed a long train of sick and baggage from Reading. The umpire in-chief was hurried in his criticisms and summing up by the necessity of the Liverpool officers to catch a certain train. The manmuvring for positions was most inter- esting and earned much praise from Colonei Marshall West. The Distress Committee. A meeting-of this committee will be held at the -Towa Hall on Monday evening when the business will include the election of chairman and deputy chairman for the ensuing year, the passing of standing orders, and the appointment of sub-committees. In his quarterly report submitted to the Inquiry and Labour sub-committee on November 22nd the inquiry officer (Mr. James Fargher) expressed regret that the various Trades Unica unemployed in the towa had not taken ahvantage of the Bureau This was a point which rather militated_ against the committee, as ha was of opinion that thd-abstentinn of these men from register- ing their names had given rise to the rumour that good class of labour was not supplied through its ageacy, and he would suggest that the various Trade Union bodies in the town he communicated with, ansl asked for their co-operation to make the bureau what it ought to be.—a supplier of all classes of labour. A statemeat of the recsipts and expenditure for the year ended 'March 31st. 1906, shows that the rats contribu- tions to the Committee from the Council amounted to Lot 18s. 2d. The expenditure incurred was :—by labour exchanges, employment registers, ard collec- tion of information, ,t 7 ss. Id. ; salaries of officers, £2O 7s. Id. ; other establishment charges, £27 leaving a debit balance of 55.--The Inquiry Officer presents his annual report, stating that from the formation of the Bureau in Decenethe-, 1905 to March 31st, 1906, the total number of persons registered was 261, and work was found for 33. From a detailed statement submitted it appears that exhaustive inquiriea were made as to the character of applicints, and numerous visits wem paid to Nirorks to ascertain if there were any vacancies. , Mr.Fargher states in conclusion that at the date of this ' report the usefulness of the Bureau has not been ' proved, but if it is borne in mind that the Bureau can I do nothing to increase the amount of employment and ' can only find workmen for those employers of labour who require them, it will probably be admitted that the aim of the Committee in establishing the Burean has been realized.

Results for December Bth. Second Division. Widnes St. Paul's 0, Halton A.F.C. 0. St. Elphins 2, Daresbury 1. Orford 0, Oughtrington Park Res. 0. Latchford Cres. 3, Sankey St. Mary's 1.

Warrington v. Liverpool City. Warrington paid their first visit to Livetpool on Saturday to play the newly-formed City Rugby Club. I The engagement was not considered in a very serious light and sever of the regularsflrst-team players were absent from the Warrington ranks. The weather was fine and dry, but the frost had made the ground rather hard. Teams: Warrington—Berry, back; Landor, Dickenson, Cart- i wright, and Brooks, three-quarter backs ; Lees and Jenkins, half-backs : Thomas. Preston, Heath, Harmer, I Foster and Belton, forwards. Llvern op I Cit —Liversedge, back; Garnett, Booth, I y half three-quarter backs; Mead and Ambrose Abel, 1, Ellis, Taylor,Jones, Lewis, 'Griffiths, and ; . Fryery forwards. Preston kicked off in a moderate sort of way, and the I Usual pack took place near midfield. Lees picked up I smartly and gave to Thomas, who simply walked in I Without the slightest opposition. Preston failed to augment, toppled dt him made the ball falling a yard short. Restarting ade an efflt to get down, but Dickenson over, and a free kick to the visitors resulted in Thomas again crossing, but Preston failed at the place. From another free kick Liversedge kicked a capital goal, and shortly afterwards made a mark, which proved useful. Play slackened off consider- ably until Brooks ran behind the posts, and Preston kicked a goal. Then Warrington took the ball on the boss and Heath crossed, Preston striking the upright. The City team made two or three sport., but Warring- ton had then well in hand, and Dickenson followed with another try on the left, no goal resulting. A free kick relieved the Liverpool position somewhat,and then Warrington were penalised. Garnett kioked to touch and Abel rushed over. Liversedge placing the ball successfully between the posts. Half-time : Warring- ton. 19 pointe ; Liverpool, 7 points. Soon after resuming Foster took advantage of a good pass by Heath to cross the home line and plant the ball behind the posts for Preston to easily add the goal points. Shortly afterwards Thomas scored again, and later Jenkins crossed, neither place kick being success- ful. Jenkins soon after trotted over the Liverpool line and planted the ball behind the posts for Thomas to goal. Brooks followed Jenkins' example directly afterwards. Harmer making a very poor effort from an easy place. Fine passing led to Brooks scoring another try, while before the end further tries were added by Preston and Heath. Final : Warrington four goals thirteen tries (47 points), Liverpool City two goals ode try (seven points).

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION WARRINGTON " A " V. WIGAN "A." Unusual interest centred in this match at Warrington on Saturday, as both teams are strong candidates for the championship of the combination, and as a result there was a large " gate." The visitors had a strong side, and aided by the wind and capable forward work the general run Of play was in the home quarters. A combined movement between Jenkins and Davies was Instrumental in Warrington forcing the visitors back; and Mason having a kick charged, matters went in favour of the " Wirepullers" until Voisey, Watkins,and Blears got away, and Knight was severely tested. Jones effected a good save from the Wigan forwards, whilst Coulthard was held up a yard inside. The visitors continued to force the pace, and the bail rarely left the home quarters. Voisey was thrown into touch at the -corner, but at half-time no score had been recorded. Turning round, the Warringtonians, with the aid of the wind, soon pressed, and from clever work by Kaye and Jenkins, Landor scored. Wigan rushed the game to half-way, only to be driven back by Knight,and another desperate raid by the visitors ended in one of the Wigan hacks having his kick charged, and Landor crossed fr..r his second try. Warrington after this last success were always masters of the situation and finally won by two tries (sis points), to Wigan" A." nil.

A SPLENDID SUCCESS IN ST. HELENS.

A notable success has been achieved in St. Helens, and the story of Mr. Talbot's experience will be eagerly read, as much may be gathered from it which will be of great use to many of us. Mr. George Talbot, 3G,Alma-street, Peasley Cross, St. Helens, was in the Army 21 years, part of which he served in India. . .

"I have no doubt it was the foreign service that was the cause of my health giving way," says Mr. Talbot. " For five years I didn't know What it was to be free from pain in the small of my back. At my work I am continually stooping, and often get damp. This used to affect my kidneys, and make me feel very bad. The kidney secretions were irregular and painful and contained a sediment. "I had doctor's medicine, but it didn't seem to do me the least good. Doan's backache kidney pills, however, have given me wonderful relief ; I am sure they have cured my kidneys, for lam so much better. ' I cannot speak too highly of Doan's pills, for I have found them to be a splendid medicine. Doan's Backache Kidney Pills are two shillings and ninepenca per box (six boxes for thirteen shillings and ninepence). Of all chemists and stores, or post free direct from Foster-McClellan Co., 8, Wells-street, , Oxford-street, London, W. Be sure you get exactly the same kind of pills that Mr. Talbot had.

WARRINGTON JUNIOR LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

The Land Question.

The members of the Junior Liberal Association bed their concluding meeting of the first part of the winter session on Monday evening when the subject under con- sideration was: " A social problem and how solved by taxing lam' values." Mr. Joseph Hawthorn presided over a fair attendance.

An address was delivered by Mr. R. OCKEL, secretary of the Warrington branch of the English League for the Taxation of Land Values, whol dealt with the Question in every interesting and instruc- tive way. Many people. he said, contended that as they had no land the question of taxing land values had no interest for them. That was far from being the case. To illustrate this in a homely way, a beggar who called to ask assistance from a believer in the " single tax," denied that the land question had anything to do with unemployment, because he himself was a builder. The ' single taxer " therefore set him to build a house on a certain piece of land but the man soon returned look- ing very scared." Why," he said "did you send me on to land that was not yours? The landlord came and said that if I did not go away immediately he would fetch the police." That showhd how the land question affected even that man. All the great social problems would be solved automatically by the taxation of land values. Mr. Ockel then showed how the value of land was, at least in part, created by the public expenditure and maintained by the annual disbursement of the rates. Every public improvement crystallised into land-value and was collected by the land- lord in tbe shape of increased rent. " The value of land 'created by the dense population of London, ' " wrote Mr. William Saunders, L.C.C., more than seen years ago, " amounts to .E11i,000,000 per annum ; and instead of retaining the value which they create, the ' dense population ' pay this sum to idle land-owners and subscribe annually £8,000,000 out of t'leir own pockets to maintain and increase the value of land." Mr. Ockel gave a striking THE LIMING OF MEADOW LAND. instance from Chicago where a quarter acre plot of land was sold in 1830, when Chicago was An impoi tent report of experiments on the liming of I a frontier post with a population of 50 souls. for 20 meadow land by Mr. Ed. As a matter of fact the taxation of land values was the only unbloody way of doing away with the House of Lords, because if they taxed land 20s. in the £ these noble gentlemen who put on airs in the House of Lords would no longer be lards and would have to set to work—which would be very healthy for them. PEACE SUNDAY.

The committee of the Peace Society have sent out letters, numbering nearly 40,000, to all ministers in charge of churches throughout the kingdom' inviting their observance of Peace Sunday on the 23rd December next. They offer, as usual, information and literature, free of char;e on application (but this year for personal use only, because of the state of their funds), and also the tervices of their Secretary and Agents to speak, lecture or preach on behalf of Peace : anct suggest a collection and the organisation of congregational societies. Address, Dr. Darby, 47, New Broad Street London. E,c,

The Examiner, Saturday, December 15, 1906.

===== 0002601_19061215_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Bewsey-road Wesleyan Churn.

• Sunday was observed as a day of misskaary inter- cession at th 3 above Church and special reference was made to the work of foreign missions by the Rev. E. J. Gawne at the morning service and the Rev. S. Pitt _in the evening.

===== 0002601_19061215_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
Wesleyan Choir Festival.

The annual choir festival in connection with Bold- street Wesleyan Church was celebrated on Sunday when large congregations assembled at the special services which were held. The preacher was the Rev. Enoch Salt of Southport,who in the morning took for his text. Colossians iii. 16, " Let the word of Christ dwell in you richly in all wisdom, teaching and admonishing one another in psalms and hymns and spiritual songs, singing with grace in your hearts to the Lord." He spoke \of the value of music and singing, and the power it exercised in congregational worship. They were also greatly indebted, he said, to hymn writers,' and among others to two of their own denomination namely, John and Charles Wesley. In the evening the rev, gentleman spoke from the words "I am come that they might have life, and that they might have it more abundantly " (St. John x. 10). As usual special atten- tion was paid to the music. In the morning Handel's "0 Father Whose Almighty power," was ably pre- sented while Mrs. Booth sang the solo from" The Messiah" "Come unto Him. In the afternoon the cantata "A Song of Thanksgiving " (Maunder), was rendered by the choir. Mr. E. J. Guest presided in the unavoidable absence of Dr. Naden. An excellent inter- pretation was given of the cantata, the choruses being well sung by the choir. The two duets were given by Miss C. Hastings and Mr. Clayton and by Mrs. Booth and Mr. Clayton, the solos by Mrs. Booth, Miss Taylor, Miss E. Taylor, and Mr. Plumpton, acd the trio by Miss Taylor, Mrs. Unsworth and Mr. Warburton. The anthem at the evening service was "I praise Thee, 0 Lord " (Mendelssohn), the solo being taken by Mr. C. H. Page. Mrs. Booth sang with mnch effect,the other solo," Lead, kindli light " (E. P. Evans). Mr. Frank Hickman presided at the organ at each services Collec- tions were made in aid of the trust funds.

===== 0002601_19061215_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
The Late Mr. Dewhurst.

Pulpit Reference by the Rector.

Preaching at the Parish Church on Sunday morning from the text " Man goeth forth unto his work and to his labour until evening." Psalm civ. 23., the Rector paid a fitting tribute to the memory of the late Mr. John Dewhurst. Mr. Dewhqrst's work, he said, was work of immense importance, the imparting of a , sound secular and religious education. He began it when he was 23 years of age, and ended it when he was 66, and it was well and faithfully done. It was import- ant that the young should be well equipped for the battle of life. If they wished to maintain their position in the world they needed to be well instructed in al/ secular knowledge. It was important also when educating the mind that the soul should be prepared for the higher life. There would be none of these terrible divisions if people were only taught the truth while they were young, when their minds were in a receptive mood. He had been looking carefully back for nearly 20 years, and the first thing which had impressed him most in Mr. D‘wharses character was his loyalty to the appointed authority,and secondly, his parental affection. Then there was his love for, and his devotion to the Church. He took great interest in the rebuilding of the church wall, in the planning of the churchyard, in playing the organ when necessary at the children's services, in the attendance of his boys, while his work in connection with the restoration of the church was worthy of mention. He was regular in his attendance at church, punctual at the Holy Communion, and devout while in the house of God. Mr. Dewhurst bad only a few days of suffering, and was quite prepared for his end. They believed it was peace, and that his soul rested with his Savionr. Let them all try to do their best, as he had done, faith- fully and coqscientiously. At the conclusion of the service the organist, Mr. Darby. gave an excellent ren- dering of the Dead March in" Saul," the congregation standing as he did so, the scene being a most impressive one.

===== 0002601_19061215_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
P.S.A. Meetings.

Mr. David Owen presided over the weekly meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society and spoke upon " Content- ment." He did not mean,he said, that they were not to have an ideal in life, but a submissive patient spirit, making their life beautiful in character. Mr. William Light, of Warrington, took for his subject "Shall we live again?" and said the infidel would reply " No," but he would say" Yes." When they considered the great mind of man, and its great capabilities, he was bound to say " Yes." Surely there was a greater mind than man who had prepared the heaven for his eternal home. Then look at the great plans for the elevation of man- kind' the great strides in commerce, arts and science,all revealing the Great Giver of every good and perfect gift, and the Great Provider for time and the great eternity. (Applause). Mr. Healey and Mr. Downes also took part in the meeting. Mr. W. H. Payton pre- sided at the organ. The secretary welcomed two new members making a total of 503. There was a very good attendance at an open meeting of the Warrington Society in the Sankey-street Co-operative Hall on Sunday. Councillor Stott referred to the selfish hoarding up of money, and stated that the protts of the earth should be shared by all people. Mr. Stott incidentally referred to a dog show held recently in London where the dogs were decked in the most expensive things whilst only a few hundred yards away men, women, and children were dying of hunger. Rev.C. Harvey Cook delivered an interesting address on the words, "Purposeful social amelioration.' He showed what a great work the members of that society could do for God and humanity if only they were purposeful. Mr. Alfred Bate rendered two violin solos. " Kirchen sire " and " Sonatina," and Miss Atherton played a pianoforte solo, each of the musical items being greatly appreciated. Mr. J. A. Heywood Iread the scripture, and prayer was offered by - Mr. R. Nelson. The President (Mr. E. Wynne-Jones) also took part in the service. \

===== 0002601_19061215_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
St Peter's Church Army Mission.

In celebration of the anniversary of this mission, special services have been held, Clptain Powell, St Margaret's, Leicester, being the special preacher. The annual tea meeting subsequently took place, and a good number partook of the tea, served in an admirable manner by the lady friends of the mission. A torchlight procession paraded the streets of the parish, and invita- tions were extended to the people to come to the mass meeting. The Bagged School Drum and Fife Band led the march, playing popular sacred tunes. The mass meeting was presided over by Councillor Steel,supported by the Vicar (the Rev. B. Percival) and the Curate (the Rev. H. Russell), and Messrs. Clayton, Knight, Hands, John Taylor, James Taylor, and J. E. Webb. The Chairman spoke in high terms of the work of the Church Army, and said how glad he was it had received , recognition by their Majesties the King and Queen, and members of the Royal Family, also by the archbishops and bishops. it was doing a magnificent work amongst the outcasts in its labour and rescue homes all over the land. its evangelists and mission sisters were working with great blessing and success amongst those unreached by the ordinary parochial agencies. (Applause.) He was glad to say that in St. Peter's parish the mission was doing an excellent work in trying to evangelize those unreached by the ordinary efforts. He appealed to all present by their prayers and practical sympathy to further the work in every possible way. In conclusion, he appealed to the meeting to make the Bishop of London's words their own resolution, " I will back up the work of the Church Army on every possible occasion." (Applause.) The Vicar expressed his gratitude for the work of the mission and spoke most highly of the Church Army officers who had worked in the parish. Captain Higson, the resident captain, was, by permission of head- quarters, to stay another year amongst them. (Applause.) Captain Powell, formerly of St. Peter's, and Captain Bourne, of Wigan also gave brief speeches, and were well received. Solos were excellently tendered by the Misses Sumner and Hill, and Messrs. Bennett, Taylor, and Thomas. The English Concertina Party gave a selection. Mr.F. Webster was the accompanist. Hymns were sung at intervals in a stirring manner.

The MAvon (Alderman Smethurst) in proposing a vote of thanks to Sir Henry Hibbort for distributing the prizes, expressed his pleasure at the increased interest which was being taken in Warrington in the education question. They would agree that it was necessary for both parents and children to give the Education Co nmittee all the assistance which they could in order that, the schools might attain that high standard of perfection which they all desired. The future of the English race would depend more upon brains than upon force, and if we were to maintain our commercial position we required a well trained com- mercial mind. It was this that the Education Com- mittee was endeavouring to supply, but their work would be of little avail unless they received the support of the parents and the children. (Applause). PADGATE.

PADGATE DAY SCILOOL.—On Thursday afternoon the prizes which had been won by the boys and girls of the above school were distributed. Many of the children's parents were present. Sir Henry Hibbert who was in the neighbourhood took the opportunity of paying the school a visit and was warmly welcomed. Messrs. Mather (District Councillor),and Allen and the Rev.W. THE REPORTS.

The Director read reports on the work of the various institutions. Regret was expressed that the number of students desiring art instruction grew less each year, and it was stated that probably the pOospects in the artistic trades were not sufncient to attract youths and girls. At the Technical Institute the committee had taken an important departure in making it compulsory upon all new students of trade subjects to take a course of study. A considerable amount of time and money had been wasted in the past by providing classes in trade-subjects for students who had not had the necessary knowledge of the related subjects to be able to follow their teacher, but the time had passed when students must be bribed to attend evening classes,-and the classes must be arranged to meet the requirements of the serious student and not the student who came to the school because it enabled him to spend an evening which might otherwise hang heavily upon his hands. There was no obstacle to a Warrington boy or girl with ability to obtain a good education. Scholarships were provided so that a child might pass from the elementary school to either the Grammar School, the Secondary school, or the evening Technical schools, and from the latter to the Technical Institute. No doubt when the time arrived, the Committee would be prepared to pro- vide scholarships to higher institutes outside the Borough, and so make the chain complete.

===== 0002601_19061215_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
SIR HENRY HIBBERT'S SPEECH.

Sir HENRY HIBBERT, who was cordially received, said he regretted exceedingly that through force of circum- stances Warrington seceded from the county and became a County Borough. He regretted it in the cause of education because to his mind the great weakness of part 2of the Education Act of 1902 was the granting of autonomy to so many authorities in Lancashire. He felt certain, that both from the educational raid the financial point of view they would have worked a great deal better if there had been one supreme authority in Lancashire for the conduct of Secondary education. Seeing that such was not to be the case, they had to do the best they could each in their respective ways. He was glad to hear from the report of the Director of Ethica- -1 tion that things in education were running so smoothly. He congratulated the students and the staff, for he was told that their successes in the examinations of the Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes were the largest in the two counties. (Applause.) With regard to the education question generally, he was a business man and he presumed he was addressing business people, so he would go down to the bed rock of the question at once. Why were they spending this money? In the administrative county, outside Government grants they were spending a million per annum on education. Public men were giving an enormous portion of their time to pushing forward the education of the people, and the reason was that they had found from experience that our business supremacy was threatened. They realized that our energy and our natural inventive genius went for very little unless they were coupled with a scientific education. They could quite understand that the United States, with its great natural ;esources, had wrested the supremacy in the iron and steel trade from us, but where our resources were as great, as in the case of Germany, they could not iiiiderstand it. Whereas some few years ago we occupied the proud position of being the greatest pro- ducers of iron and steel and their manufactures, to-day the United States, by virtue of its great natural resources, headed the list, Germany came second, and the United Kingdom third. So it was in an enormous number of other trades—in fact in nearly all those trades where science seemed to underlay their foundations.lt therefore became imperative,that if we were in future to secure due and adequate employrneat for our people, we must educate them better. That was the reason why the administrative county of Lancashire was spending so much money on education, why the Corporation was following suit, and their business men were giving of their energy and time to support a national cause. (Applause.) What were the difficulties in the way of the development of education ? A leading educationist from one of the great cities recently told him that• whereas in his city only 800 boys and girls attended the municipal secondary school, in a similar town in the United States there were 8000 pupils. He instanced the case of a washerwoman in the United States who earned 20s. a week and yet kept her daughter at school till she was sixteen or seventeen years of age. He asked her this pregnant question : "How can you afford to keep her there?" and the reply was "I cannot afford not to keep her there." (Applause.) As a contrast, some years ago, a boy competed for a .C3O exhibition given by the , Manchester City Council and won it, but his father would not allow him to take it, because the amount would not compensate for the loss of the boy's wages. This might have been all right many years ago when there were not the same incentives to education and when there was not the same command of public money for the purpose. Our secondary education on which so much depended was to a very large extent neglected, save on. the part of those who, blessed with this , world's goods, were able to pay the comparatively ' high fees. Things had now altered completely. In the Lancashire County area it was possible by means of scholarships and exhibitions for a boy of ability to 'mount from the elementary school right up to the University with comparatively little cost to his parents (Applause.) There were

===== 0002601_19061215_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
SEVERAL _WEAK POINTS

in our armour and the principal -;;;;;i: of all was that to which the Directors had alluded—the unprepared- ness of students who sought admission to the evening classes. They had had considerable experience of that in the county, notably in the plumbing classes. Appren- tices who came to enter were so imperfectly educated that they could not take advantage of the scientific instruction which it was absolutely necessary they should obtain. He (Sir Henry) was a great admirer of the elementary system of education in this country and he considered that, so far as it went, there was no better elementary education in any other country. (Applause.) Unfortunately, however, according to the byelaws of the public authorities children left, school when they were thirteen or fourteen years of age. That would not matter if they immediately went to an evening continuation school; but what did they do ? Those children were educated at an expense of anywhere from twelve to fourteen millions per annum, but in consequence of leaving school at so early an age and loitering about the streets until they were about twenty years of age, a large proportion of that money was absolutely wasted. (Applause.) A report submitted by the Director of Education at Wigan showed that the average age of students attending the evening classes there was over twenty. What was the condition of the student who left the elementary school at fourteen and did not go in for any serous study until he was twenty ? The condition was one c% ith which all who had to do with education were thoroughly conversant. They had to start de nom snd in addition to wasting a portion of the twelve to fourteen millions of which he had spoken, they wasted a large proportion of the money devoted to higher education, of which they had far too little to spend. All the ratepayers complained of the education rate, but he wanted them to put the blame on the right shoulders. It was not their education authority ; it was the

===== 0002601_19061215_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
FITHERS AND MOTHERS OF LANCASHIRE

who were to blame for this wasted money. If the people took advantage of the facilities which were offered to them, performed a parent's duty and saw that when their childen left the elementary school, if for no other reason than to keep them out of mischief they were sent to evening classes, the result from a financial point of view would be infinitely superior to what it was to-day; land from a moral point of view it would do an enor- mous amount of good. (Applause). He would conclude by quoting the words of Mr. Haldane, one of the great- est authorities on higher education in the country : "Educate your people and you reduce to insignificance the problems of intemperance, housing, and the well- being of the masses." (Loud applause.) THE WARRINGTON COMMITTEE'S WORK.

The Chairman, said he thought that in Warring- I ton they had every reason for congratulation. During the banding of Solomon's temple no sound of axe or hammer was heaid and that seemed to fittingly repre- sent the work of the Education Committee. During the past year the country had been torn from end to end with a fierce religious controversy, but no echo of the sectarian strife came within the walls of the council

THE BEAMONT SCHOOL. Selection of Headmaster and Headmistress. COMMITTEE REFUSE TO CONFIRM.

The managers of the school appointed by the Educa- tion Committee for the purpose of selecting a headmaster and headmistress for the " Beamont " Council School met on Tuesday afternoon at the Education Offices, Sankey-street. There were originally over 150 applicants for the headmastership and the meeting had to make a selection from twelve candidatei. Ultimately Mr. Mascn, of Eccles, was appointed headmaster, and Miss Mary E. Taylor, of the Fairfield Upper Girls' School, headmistress. The salaries have been fixed until March 31st, 1903, to be at the following rates :—Headmaster (boys' department), £225 a year ; headmistress (girls' depart- ment), £l2O. After the date mentioned the salaries will be fixed according to the committee's scale on the average attendance for the year ending March 31st, 1303. The staff of St. George's Infant Council School will be transferred to the infants' department of the new school at their present salaries with one exception. This is Miss Ellen Shaw, who is to be appointed one of the five assistant certificated mistresses in the girls' depart- ment of the new schools. At a meeting of the School Management Commit e on Thursday the recommendation of the managers bf the " Beamont " Council school for the appointment of the head teachers was considered, and it was resolved, that the recommendation of the managers of the " Beamont " Commit school be approved and adopted with the exception of that portion relating to the appointment of Mr. W. Mason as head master of the boys' department.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE BUSINESS. ____

The ordinary meeting of the Education Committee will be held on Monday at 6 p.m., when the above business will be considered, together with the business given below. SNICONDARI- AND TECIINICAL ACCOMMODATION. The School Management Committee on Thursday met the representatives of the Board of Education and,after discussing the means of meeting the growing needs for additional accommodation for the Secondary school and Technical Institute, the further consideration was post- poned to the next meeting. THE QUESTION OF PEES. Communications having been subtuittod from tbb Board of Education, intimating that they cannot alter their decision declining to consent tb the charge of fees at the Bearnont Council, it was resolved, that this Com- mittee regret the decision of the Board of Education. since it will render the charging of fees in other schools henceforth inequitable, and will necessitate the abolition of fees throughout the town and a consequent heavy addition to the burden of the rates : and that it be recommended that the Local Authority no longer sanction the charging of fees in auy of the public elementary schools in Borough.

PRESENT-COUPONS.

Present-day competition is responsible for bringing into vogue a great many schemes for the sale of goods, some of which are open to serious objection, both from the point of view of the consumer and of legitimate competition. In this category must be placed the system of coupon-present giving which is growing now to such alarming dimensions that if the present rate of growth is maintained there will hardly he a household commodity, the purchase of which does not carry with it the doubtful privilege of obtaining free of charge a picture, a box or chocolates, a photograph, some knives or forks, or other articles. Now surely it is time to cry halt and ask whence all this is tending. Who is to benefit by it ? Certainly not the consumer. When Nlrs. Janes purchases her groceries she buys what she 1 desires, and it is not in her interest that she should have included in the price the cost of a " present "which she neither asked for nor requires,and in a large number of cases she does not trouble to obtain. The system also has the effect of setting up a competition in present-giving among manufacturers with the restlt that the articles sold are reduced in quality in propor- tion as the "presents" are increased in value. The whole system is bad from top to bottom, and it is very much to be regretted that so many manufacturers of cocoa, which is so largely advertised, should have adopted it. It is interesting to note, however, that one large cocoa firm, viz., Van Houton's, so far from adopting a similar system, are making a dead set against it, as will be seen from their advertisements.

Annual Appointments,

The q:uarterly meeting in connection with the War- rington Wesleyan Methodist Circuit was held at Bold- street Church on Thursday. There wes an unusually largeattendance presided over by the Rev. Sidney Pitt, who was supported by the other circuit ministers. The Circuit Stewards' financial statement showed that the income for the quarter had exceeded the ex- penditure by about £32. The Rev. SIDNEY. Prrr presented the numerical returns showing a membership of SBO with 80 on trial— an increase of 11. A number of the representatives made feeling refer- ences to the death of Councillor Walter Brough who was a prominent member of the quarterly meeting. A record of his services was ordered to be placed on the minutes and a letter of sympathy to be sent to the The circuit stewards (Mr. J. G. Wiihinshaw and Mr. John Upson) were thanked for their services, special reference being made to Mr. Withinshaw who retired after four years of office. Several members spoke of the good work he had done. Mr. JOHN UPON was elected senior circuit steward and Mr. R. H. Meldrum, of Lynitu, was appointed junior steward. ;,‘• The following additional appointments were made : —auditors, Messrs. William Silcock and I. E. Barlow : educational secretary, Rev. J. Manning ; circuit chapel secretary, Mr. F. Harris; auditors of foreign missionar y accounts, Messrs. I. E. Barlow and F. Pendlebury ; secretary of the quarterly meeting, Mr. William S ilcock.

A vote of thanks was passed to the local preachers for their voluntary services at the various churches during the past year, and it was decided that the special collection for the Local Preachers' Mutual Aid Associa- tion should be MAde throughout the circuit in June instead of in August as formerly. There was a discussion on the Silver-street mission where lantern services have been held for some years on Sunday evenings. Hitherto this work has been privately managed by a small committee but it is now' oEcially recognized by the quarterly meeting and the services are to be placed on the plan and preachers appointed in the usual way. Thanks were accorded to thG editors and secretary of the " Church Record."

It was reported that the money necessary to the engagement of a supernumerary minister for Lymm had been guaranteed with the exception of about £lO per annum. This balance was guaranteed by members of the meeting so that as soon as a suitable minister can be secured the appointment will be made.

Mess, nag they not lutetium to 'encourage him. "It is almost as good as .an acceptance," he said to himself, and his eyes shone, "and yet not half so good: lam sitting here alone now; when she has accepted me my world of joy and gladness will be fully peopled." Quite unconsciously, while he waited,„he sang softly to himself, watching the birds who already had housekeeping ideas in their little heads; the bees that flew hither and thither in the sunlight looking for flowers, revelling in the kiss of the baby breeze that touched his cheek gently as it passed him by. And then he heard her coming, and rose. Instinctively he put his hand upon his hat and stood bare-headed waiting. Ile could hear that she came slowly, hesitatingly, as though half- afraid, and his man's soul laughed in triumph. He was glad she was just a little afraid of love, though he would rejoice also when the fear passed. He had been timid himself. he trembled now. The woman of many experiences meets a new lover boldly. Paul Clifford did not at all wish for it woman versed in love's subtleties. 'l7his maiden whom he loved was as a resebud towards which the hand of the miler had never ' been stretched before—all the happier lie that this was so. Some men would have rushed to meet her. She had told him to wait. and he waited. Besides, was conse;olr, fully conscious, that already i• was a triumph upon his part, is surrender upon hers, that She should come. Once lie saw her, once their eyes met, his limbs would, perhaps, prove traitor to his will ancl hurry him towards her. Now, in perfect happiness, he waited. And then she came, came with a shy smile and downcast eyes. and he was beside her. "I can't think," she said, softly, "what you have to say to me." He kept her hand in his. "Oh, Olive," he said, and there was love and reproach in his voice, "surely, surely, you must knoss. can't keep it to myself any longer. All night long I have been wanting to tell you, dearest, I love you. I want you to be my wife. Will you? Will you?" His arm was round her, his head bent close to , hers; surely, as she turned her face upward to his, her lips so temptingly close, she must have known he would kiss her! "If you want me," she said—"if you really, really, truly want me." The starlings and sparrows, the blackbirds and thrushes had the conversation for a while to themselves, the breeze laughed sympathetically, the sunshine grew brighter,, while young love expressed itself otherwise than by words, and sass well content to do so. "We will be married very soon," Paul said after a time. "There is nothing to wait for. You will let me tell Jack to-day, dearest?" Bike looked dubious. She was woman enough to wish the news of her engagement to have its full importance. It was hardly likely it would on the day of her brother's wedding. "Oh, not to-day, I think," she returned. "Wait until they have come back from their honeymoon. To-day he will take interest in no one but Celia. That is natural, I think. Supposing—supposing it were your wedding- day "—she coloured adorably—"do you think it would matter to you very much whether others were happy, new-plighted lovers or no ?" He laughed heartily. "Not a bit," he answered— "not a little tiny bit. Then, sweetheart, we will keep it tosourselves until they return, and very soon after that we will go through the same ceremony that is to 'occupy their attention to- day. We, love one another; we want to be together. It will be more than beautiful to be together, and so you will marry me soon?" The assertion was a question. Olive, grown bolder now, looked up at him with a half-saucy, half-quizzical smile, then drew away from him quickly. Early as it was others were about; others were making their way to the rose garden. They could hear voices, men's voices, talking. At first they could not recognise the words, though Olive detected her brother's voice; then a merry laugh came to them. "That's settled, Jack, old chap," a strange tone said. "You've got a dragon in the shape of a housekeeper, it seems, so why should I not stop on ? I don't want to make myself unpleasant —it's the last thing I ever have done, I hope, under any circumstances but I'm bound to claim your hospitality for a week or two. You are afraid, I know, on account of • your sister, but, believe me, lam not a marrying mats Be she beautiful as Venus, or, fascinating as Cleopatra, she will not attract me." Paul Clifford half-started to his feet, a hot flush swept his face. Olive laughed, shook her head, and putting out her hand, restrained him. "Dear boy," she whispered, "we are not supposed to hear. Besides, you don't want this, impudent individual, whoever he may be, to fall in love with me, do you ? Or to turn into a marrying man for my sake ? Besides, lain neither as beautiful as Venus nor fascinating as Cleopatra. Shall we go and see who it is ? it would be better, perhaps." A look of almost comical displeasure crossed Paul's face. It seemed to him he could have sat there for ever, perfectly happy and contented, and now Jack—who certainly, being a lover himself, might have known better—had-brought someone to disturb them. Being an ardent and impatient lover, he did not do his future brother- in-law the justice of remembering that he had not the slightest idea the }slung people were there. He bent low over Olive. He kissed her again, although the footsteps were very near. "Wouldn't it be better to tell Jack now ? " he asked. "I think, Olive, I should like him to know." "And rob my triumph of half its glory!" she said. "Oh, you don't understand that it is a triumph for me to have *on your love, and perhaps I'm foolish to let you know I think so, but I do. • I wouldn't exchange lovers with any woman in the world. No, we won't tell him. Of course, if he chooses to guess—" They came face to face with the two men. The stranger had his arm thrust through Jack's was laughing all over his handsome face, evidently enjoying something that amused him very much in the situation. To all appearances, he might have been the coming happy bridegroom of the day. "You are up early, Olive," Jack said to his sister, and smiled at Paul Clifford. "Couldn't you sleep because to-day, fc/ the first time, you are to be bridesmaid?" She smiled back at Jack, with an utter absence of embarrassment which amused Paul 'Clifford, and with a saucy glance at the hand- some stranger. Paul Clifford himself felt awkward and proud—a detected criminal and a triumphant hero in one. The complex feelings of the amalgamation were not wholly pleasurable. "I could not sleep for worrying about you," she said, saucily; "for wondering how I could prevent your making blunders as you wended your way through the intricacies of the Marriage Service. I said to myself, He will surely lose the ring. He'll answer, I renounce them all, instead of / will, when he's asked if he will take this woman to his wedded wife. He'll be guilty of every absurdity possible, and I shall have to stand by and listen, powerless to help.' 1 And .so, sleep being an impossibility to me, and the morning inviting, I came out." Both Jack and his companion laughed. "And , Paul," Jack said, "was he also all anxiety with regard to to-day's ceremony ? " Paul looked very hard at the stranger. "I couldn't sleep," lid answered. "I came on the off-chance of seeing Olive in the garden." "And the off-chance came off," the stranger said, with a smile. "You were monstrously i lucky. Jack, old man, introduce me." The tone of the stranger grated upon everyone. Both Jack and Paul frowned. Olive drew hersel' up slightly. She had never seen this man before. She wondered who he could be. But so far as Jack was concerned there was no help for it. This man, it was true, was in his power, but doubly was he in the power of this man. He forced a smile as he looked at the young lovers. "I had a very pleasant surprise, he said. "Early this morning as rattle of stones son my window aroused me. I opened it, looked out, and a voice I had not heard for long greeted me. Olive "—he looked directly at his sister—"you must remember to have heard me mention my old friend Vernon Salisbury? This is he, but just returned from Australia. Hearing quite accidentally of my coining marriage, he, like thekgood fellow he is, ran down to take part in it. Mr. Vernon Salisbury—my sister. Mr. Clifford —Mr. Vernon Salisbury." Olive held out her hand. "I don't remember to have heard ou. mention, your friend," she ' said, "but lam' very pleased to see him. I hope" —there was mischief in her eyes—"he won't feel ! it necessary to run away from us to-night. If he would care to see the country, Jack, there is no reason why he should not stay? Mr. Salisbury, you must not think that I'm saving an onecus-

'to io is • The House of Lords has reversed the West Riding decision on appeal and decided that under the Act of 1902, it is permissible to pay for religious instruction in voluntary schools out of the rates. The position is thus "as you were." *s* The disgusting habit of spitting in the double-deck tramcars is becoming very prevalent, despite the notices forbidding it, Smokers now have comfortable accommodation and they should not abuse it. We hope the Committee will prosecute other offenders. Two widely opposite views prevailing in regard to education tvere pithily expressed by Sir Henry Hibbert, when he said some people looked upon it as a heavenly goddess and others as a nodlch cow. * * Much public speaking seems to have impaired Sir Henry Hi bbert's memory. In opening his speech on Thursday evening he expressed his delight at address- ing a meeting for the first time in this town. He must have overlooked the fact that he spoke at the Training College Prize Distribution in July. Or perhopshe was under the impression that the institu- tion was not within the borough boundary. - Trenchant criticism of Mr. Andrew Carnegie and his "generosity " was forthcoming from Sir W. J. Sinclair at the annual dinner of the Manchester Aberdeen University Club on Wednesday even- ing. He considered it a humiliation to Scot- land that graduates were accepting their fees from Mr. Carnegie. Never was the money of a millionaire so thoroughly wasted as the money of that • Scottish American. He was demoralising university teaching in Scotland, lowering the quality of the men who went to the universities, and throwing his money away on free libraries, for which purpose he was fifty years too late, because any working man with a week could make a library from his sayings in tobacco in the course of two or three months that would make him perfectly independent of Carnegie and his free libraries.
